PT,EASANT PARAGRAPHS.

RECORDS are like wills—as soon as they are made, there is an effort to break them.

_ "FAINT heart never won fair lady," without considerable assistance on her part. ALL things come to those who'know when to stop waiting.

- . be a good thing if people could not play the violin uni.'l they know how. No one is supe,..:-'+;nus when it comes to receiv- ing the baker's dozen. WHEN a man's good deeds speak for themselves, he should not allow his voice to drown theirs. THERE are dozens of remedies for rheumatism, and it usually lasts long enough to give its victim a chance to try them all. HARD as it is to climb up in the world, it racks us a good deal worse to climb down again. IT happens frequently that the prophet who is without honour in his own country can't afford to go abroad. "WHY are you sobbing, my little boy?" "Cause I'm ten years old tomorrer, an' fadder pro- mised me a pair of long pants ! " "But why those idle tears?" "'Cause mudder said times were hard, an' she's goin' ter cut down a pair uv me sister's bloomers fer me." FIRST LITTLE Boy: "My sister wears a number two shoe." Second Little Boy: " Booh ! That's nothing. Mine wears a number six." DEALER: "Would you like me to show you some furn iture of the style of Louis Fourteenth?" Mr. New: " 'Tain't high enough! Show us some of at least Louis Thirtieth." BARNEY: "Say, Phelim, what's a ventrilo- quist?" Phelim :•" He's a lad phat stands on one side av the room and talks to himself from the other."

NEXT to a railroad track, the small' boy prefers the unfloored second storey of an unfinished house as a playground. THE reason so many humbugs gain fame in these days is that the ladder has been superseded by the elevator. A LAMP always stands in its own light, which probably accounts for its being so often "turned down." THE second baby may weigh three pounds more than the first without causing half as much excite- ment. MANY people who profess to believe in the theory of the greatest good to the greatest number, also believe that the greatest number is No. i. KIND LADY: "Now, you will promise me that you won't spend this money on liquor ? " Tramp: "I promise, mum—and me word is as good as me bond ! " _ _

A WORK OF FICTION.—Johnny: "I don't believe this is a true story." Tommy: "Why not ? " .Johnny "It doesn't end right. It's a story about a fight between a man and a lion, and the man gets killed by the lion." BRAVE IN HER GENERATION. —"I am not afraid." The tall, slender girl laughed at the tempest. "I have nothing to fear," she cried, as a great sea swept her from the ship into the seeth- ing waters. "My hair curls naturally." LITERARY fame consists in having a great many people know that you have written something which they have not read. No matter whether a man's life results in abso- lute failure or complete success, plenty of people who had predicted it can always be found. IF, as has erroneously been said, we really "profit by our errors," every man-jack of us would be declaring dividends daily. THE average impressionist is not so bad as the picture he's painted. AIRS. HASHLEIGH : "Such a gentleman, that Mr. Hattie was! Never complained if he had no towels, never sneered at the food, or made remarks about the meals being late. LITTLE BROTHER IN MISERY. —Hostess : "I wonder why your little brother seems so restless and uncomfortable?" Little Ethel: "I guess it's 'cause his hands is clean." ECHOES OF THE WEDDING.—" WS all nonsense, dear, about wedding-cake. I put an enormous piece under my pillow and dreamed of nobody." " Well ?" he wins. TEACHER: "If a person is eighteen years old to- day, how old will that person be ten years hence?" Bright Boy: "Please, sir, is that person a man or a woman?" ON HISTORIC GROUND.—" Yes, sir, this is the place where the battle was fought." " Have you any relics of it ? " "Yes, sir. John, mould the gentleman about twenty bullets, an' tell the black- smith to hammer out a bayonet quick." THE chestnut producer sidled up to the clever man and said, "I've cust read a curious thing about Australian ants. They sit on trees and bark, and many of them weigh a pound." The clever man guffawed quietly—" The last time I heard about those ants they were Indian ones. They may weigh a pound, but I'm hanged if they bark.' And they carried him off foaming. WENT TO AN OCULIST.—Friend "Did you go to that fashionable oculist, as I suggosted ? Near- sight: "Yes he examined my eyes, and gave me a piece of paper showing the sort of glasses I needed." "Why don't you get the glasses?" "No money left."

" CA.PITAL" CHAI.

Lord James of Hereford curtailed his Christmas I recess in the country in order to be at hand in Lon- don should there be any fresh development in con- nection with the shipping strike. His lordship has been in continuous correspondence with the Pro- vost of Glasgow and the Lord Mayor of Belfast,and as soon as it is seen there is the slightest possibility of a settlement being arrived at, Lord James will summon the meeting of delegates of masters and men to reassemble at Glasgow. Wherever friendly advice is needed, Lord James is at the disposal of both engineers and shipbuilders, and both admit that his action has been most impartial.

Little has been heard of the unemployed this winter so far. It is satisfactory to learn, on the authority of the " Labour Gazette," that as com- pared with last year there is a distinct improve- ment in the labour market. Still, there is great silent suffering and poverty in London. The esti- mate of one of the labour associations is that there are n3t fewer than 100,000 men out of employment.

As a contribution to the proposed taxation of cycles, Mr. Howard Vincent, who, with ad- mirable discretion, has carefully guarded himself from any expression of opinion either for or against the tax, has collected some information as to the yield of a similar impost in France. French cyclists are taxed to the extent of 9s. per year. From this source the yield for the first complete year—namely, IB94—was nearly £BO,OOO, of which about .£lB,OOO was returned to the local authori- ties, thus giving them a direct interest in the levy. It is expected that for the year which is just clos- ing the total yield will show a substantial increase over that of 1894. Such figures seem to point to a source of revenue in this country capable of great possibilities, but any proposal in the direction of taxing cycles will meet with the most strenuous opposition. Although lately cycling has received the approbation of the upper circles, it is essenti- ally a pastime of the people,who would resent any legislative interference with it. Their interests will, no doubt be sufficiently safeguarded. Cyclists form no inconsiderable portion of the voters in many of the constituencies, and be would be a bold member who elected to disregard their views.

In the best informed circles the general view at present is that the position of affairs in the Trans- vaal is, for the moment, much more alarming than the threatened trouble with the United States. It is no longer a secret that a very large amount of ammunition has found its way from this country to the supporters of the National Union in the Transvaal,and every preparation was, it is believed, made some months ago for any possible emergency. There is a rumour in Downing-st. that a special Cabinet Council will be held shortly to consider the position of affairs as affecting British subjects.

When Mr. Chamberlain, having marked out plenty of work to occupy the Colonial Office, went away for a quiet rest, he hardly reckoned on such a stirring question awaiting his return, which was fixed for next week. It was then supposed that the arrangements for carrying an elecric cable via Canada to Anstralia, passing only through British. territory, would be the first scheme demanding his attention, as Sir Charles Tupper returns in a few days from the Dominion, where he has had long conferences on the subject. lam told that this cable scheme has not caught on very firmly in Canada. Australia will be asked to nominate two representatives to the Conference to be held at the Colonial Office early in February.

The annual County Ball at Hatfield House, on January Bth, is this year to be more resplendent than ever. The house party alone is to muster over a hundred persons, many county magistrates with their friends being invited to stay over the festivities. Besides this. a special train for guests is to leave London in the evening, and to return early the next morning with thwe who cannot get beis in Hatfield. The best musicians have been engaged, and the supper is to be a notable effort. The whole ot the old Elizabethan house is to be thrown open, and its store of curiosities will be on view. People are making extraordinary exertions to obtain invitations, there !being much heartburn- ing in" Society "among those who fear they are to be left out in the cold.

The American Department of La'lour, which has issued so many useful reports, has now begun to publish a monthly bulletin, which is more in nature of a collection of short reports on strikes in the United States, England, Austria, Italy and France, in 1894, a paper on public debt in America, and short articles on other subjects. Mention is made of the fact that there are now 28 State bureaux of Labour statistics in the United States. There is a Bureau of Industries in Canada, a Department of Labour in New Zealand, Belgium and France have Labour bureaux, and the French one issues a monthly bulletin. Germany has had a Labour Statistical Department since 1891, and Switzerland a Secretariat Ouvrier, since 1886. This official is employed by the Federation of Labour organisations and is partly paid by the Government. Ihe.f.e,with our own Labour Department, completes the list of official agencies for the collection of Labour sta- ti,tics.

The other day we'heard of the laudable attempt of Mr. Rhodes to introduce English singing birds into South Africa. One shipment has arrived in excellent condition, and another is to follow. The little ' songsters are being acclimatised in Mr. Rhode's own aviary. Another interesting move on the part of the Cape Premier is the dispersal of his flock of Angora goats. He announces that he will part with most of the goats at cost price to any farmers who will make good use of them, and he has sent a couple as pioneers to Mashonaland.

Everybody belives that Lord Londonderry, like Lord George Hamilton, will in course of time re- -I,quinquish the chairmanship of the London School Board on account of being called to high office in the State. The marked prominence which he gave to foreign affairs in his speech at Tudhoe is pro- bably the cause of the curious and startling report that he is destined in more serene times to be offered the post of Foreign Secretary. There may be no limit to the ambition of Lord Londonderry, but it is absolutely incredible that a man of his slender experience should be invited to undertake obligations which tax the abilities of the mcst brilliant and accomplished statesmen:

I learn on good authority that the vacancy in the Whip's office, caused by the succession of the Mar- quis of Carmarthen to the peerage, will be offered to Sir Herbert Maxwell, M.P. for Wigtownshire. Sir Herbert was a Junior Lord of the Treasury in the last Conservative Government, and did hard work for the party during the time it was in op- position.

LYNCHING AEFAIR IN KENTUCKY.

TWO PERSONS MURDERED.

Reuter's New York correspondent telegrapns :—" A telegram from:Lebanon, Kentucky, states that a mob at that place has bnrned to death a widow named West and shot a grey-haired man named D-vers, with whom she was living. Several months ago Dsvers shot Mrs. ftvers, and afterwards took up his residence in Mrs. West's house. The matter created great indignation in the neighbourhood, and the tragedy now reported is attributed to the action of Mr. West's relatives. The mob, who were not masked, refused to pay any heed to the pleadings of the victims and of Mrs. West's little children, and also an appeal made on hehalf of the woman on the ground that she was enceinte. The affair is expected to lead to further violence.

A BORN FINANCIER.

A country parson wrote to Barney Birnato the other day ; " My aim has always been invest ment.not specula- tion. When yQur bank came out I regarded its shares as an investment, and purchased four hundred of them at four pounds, sinking my little all in them, and a good deal more. They have now fallen to two pounds, and I am undone. I cannot face my parisn as a bankrupt,and what am Ito do ? I throw myself upon your mercy." Barnato was deeply moved by the ai p•-al. and replied that he would buy back the shares at tour pounds. On receipt of this reply, the guileless parson wired to his brokers to " buy four hundred shares Barnato BArik stock at two pounds, and send them round to Barnato Brothers, who will give you four pounds for them."

TilL AMINER,

LADIES' LETTER.

ME DEAREST NELLIE,

There are any number of materials to choose from for your Christmas evening gown, and they are all of them tempting. Lovely silks, satins, and velvet rank first for dinner and reception toilettes. There is a perfect craze for shot effects in velvet and silks, new tints being employed, such as pink toned with yellow, pale blue with grass green. Thin woollen materials in light shades of colour are being used to make inexpensive dresses for young girls, while muslins, silks, satins, spangled tulles, and gauzes are among the novelties for ball gowns. Beginning with dinner and recep- tion dresses, we find the skirts are invariably plain. They are lined through with stiff muslin, and set out in wide pleats at the back. Trains are considered quite out of fashion; all the new skirts just scrape the ground, and that is all. Much depends on the successful trimming of evening bodices when the skirts are so simple. Thick silks, velvets, and brocade should be made up in a stretched shape, so that they fit the figure accurately. The basque should be so short that it only just covers the waistband. The front of the bodice ually has some fulness. With evening dresses the coiffure is, of course, of supreme importance. In arranging your hair you should not simply borrow a coiffure that looked well on someone else; it may not be at all suited for you. Choose a style of hairdressing which harmonises with your face. The piquant smiling face, with nose retrousse, for instance, should be wreathed with curly locks, while the woman with classical features should adopt the Grecian style of hairdressing. Dark hair does not, as a rule, look well if too much curled or crimped. On the other hand, those who possess golden or reddish hair should wave it on each side, and puff it high in a fluffy mass. One of the most fashionable styles is an arrangement known as the artistic neglige style. This is really a revival of and old-time mode. The hair is carefully parted in the middle waved at the sides, and brought down over the ears. It is confined in a loose knot at the back, and fancy combs are thrust through the hair on either side to set it out and give a full, bushy appearance. A few little curls are allowed to stray over the forehead.

Then there is the Grecian coiffure I mentioned a moment ago; and this is more particularly suited to pale complexions. It would not, of course, suit any but refined features, so we are not likely to have our head-dresses copied by our Mary Janes. Strings of pearls are used in these coiffures, and they look very well in dark hair. These are not the only ornaments, of course, but with this coiffure no elaborate jewellery should be used, as it would be quite out of taste. Braided jackets a la militaire are becoming very popular. This is another feature of the Trilby craze which we have all got so badly now. Brown jackets are the ones which are usually braided— the braid being black, of course,—and it is used all down the front of the garment. though not quite so profusely as on actual military jackets. But the best way to know how much to use is to look at Mr. Du Maurier's drawing in his book. The

jacket I am thinking of just now has the cuffs braided too, the trimming reaching to the elbow, above which the sleeves are of the balloon character, A pretty hat to wear with this jacket is of black vel- vet trimmed with black feathers and black and white ribbon. A " Beefeater " would do equally well. There are some very charming ornaments for evening wear to be seen just now. The new floral garnitures are absolutely lovely, and the prettiest consist of a narrow wreath for edging a square or round d6colletage, these having natural-looking little trails depending nearly to the waist at front and back. A necklet of the same flowers encircles the throat, and a half wreath, with a fascinating aigrette, is prepared for the hair, and is adjusted round the left side of the coiffure. Neapolitan violets are first favourites, but tiny pink roses come next, while for Hebutantes there are snow- drops, daisies, or jonquils, all in the same style. Children's hats in cream silk, plush edged, and trimmed with Thibet, Persian lamb, or lambs-wool, also of cream colour, are perfectly irresistible this season, so, too, are some of the white and cream felts, for they are treated in the same manner and look exceedingly well with the upper brim edged

with a 2-inch band of Thibet. As to dresses, the accordion pleat is very popular, and very large yokes are worn. A very stylish accordion pleated costume has velvet yoke and adjustable collar embroidered with silk. It may be made in sage green, crimson and new blue wool. Your loving

Dishes of gold and silver used in table se' vice in 903 B.C. were found at Troy by Dr. Sehliemann. One of these was about th.e size now employed.

Perfumes are injurious to the senses of smell. By their use the secretory glands are weakened and overtaxed, and the hearing, the throat, and lungs, and the seine of smell become affected.

It is said that the finest diamond ever found in Africa was discovered at Jagersfontein, in the Transvaal, on the 26th ult. Its weight is 634 carats, and its value when cut is estimated at £300,000.

. Some Good Eye Hints.

Dr. Sterling Ryerson Writes in the Canadian " Lancet" that shortness of sight is se essentially due to the abuse of the eye that he is constantly obliged to say "don't "to his patients and has formulated his prohibi- tions as follows :—Don't read in railway trains or vehi- cles in motion. Don't read laying down in a constrained position. Don't read by firelight, or twilight. Don't read by a flickering gaslight or candlelight. Don't read books printed en thin paper. Don't read books which have no space between the lines. Don't read for more than fifty minutes without stopping, whether the eyes are tired or not. Don't hold the reading close to the eyes. Don't study at night but in the morning, when you are fresh. Don't select you own glasses at the out- set. These rules, though some appear obvioug, are commonly sinned against. As a general rule, whatever tends to increase the quantity of blood in the organ favours the defect,and leads in extreme cases to detach- ment of the retina and blindness.

Crystal Heinz Fruits.

Few confections are more delicious than candied fruit. and few sweetmeats are more expensive. They. can be prepared at about half the cost, however, at home, if care is taken. Cherries, currants, pineapples, apricots, pears, and peaches are best experimented upon. The two former can he used in bunches; the pineapple is sliced across the fruit, each piece being a good quarter inch thick; apricots are cut onte side anti the stone slipped out, while pears and peso -11/011143 halved, and, of course, peeled. Make a very thick syrup, pound for pound, adding for each pound a small cup of water. Boil the sugar first, then drop in fruit, and when they have boiled clear take e;ut and drain from the syrup. If the cherries are stoned (the red oxhearts make the finest being not so sweet as the white, and without the rank tartness of the sour red ones),it is nice to string them on A broom splint, as they can be more cleverly handled. Sprinkle liberally with powdered sugar, lay on a sieve. and set the fruit in a warm oven. In two hours turn the fruit, sprinkle with sugar again. Keep this up until the sugar is all dripped out. Of no account have the oven hot, as it will dry the fruit and leave it like so much leather. And, of course, the fruit must be laid in single rows when drying. When the juice has evaporated and the sugar has formed a glazed surfacedout away in boxes in a dry place. Waxed paper should be laid between each laver. A bureau drawer is as good a place as any to keep them.

Artistic Floor-Staining. Floor-staining insteAd of carpets has become so popu- lar that the following few hints may probably be found useful. The floor should be stained brown in the usual way, and a diamond pattern, faur inches across and three inches from point to point, carried down the centre and painted over with black enamel. You must cut your diamonds out of stiff cardboard and keep them on the floor while you stain round them. It is necessary to be careful that your cardboard's diamonds are per- fect in shape, otherwise the pattern will not look well. When you have finished the middle of the border, cut some of your diamonds in half and make a border with them round the sides of the room, the points being turned inwards tewards the diamonds in the middle. Of course, this style of decoration can be carried out in a variety of ways. For instance, the green malachite stain looks quite charming when relieved by a huge red :seroTl down the centre; especially if matting is used as the floor covering. It brightens it up wonderfully; besides which, it wears very well, particularly if a coat of oak vanish is bestowed upon it when it is throug,hly dry. It is well to remember that green also looks very well wi th matting, or with a plain felt as a foil in the middle of your room. It is not advisable, however, to use it with a Brussels or tapestry carpet of different hues. These details require careful thinking out beforehand, or instead of improvement you will vulgarise the whole.and the colouring is everything in a home. It must he har- monious to produce that sense of brightness and beauty which is the aim of every home-loving woman. Much can be done with a few pence, and it is want of energy, as a rule, more than want of means, that makes the muddled sitting-room an eyesore instead of a relief to tired workers on their return from the day's labour.

Pearls Without Price.

Casual mention has been made of the beantiful pearls which Mrs. William K. Vanderbilt gave her daughter, Miss Consuelo,when she became Duchess of Marlborough but nothing like justice has ever been done to a c,llec- don which is undonbtedly the finest and most costly in the world. The pearls originally consisted of two strings one of these being historic and a part of the once glorious strand of Catharine of Russia. This string was about two yards long,and while the nearls are well matched,of great size and of good " skin" or brilliancy, they are nothing to be compared to the second string, which Wil- liam K. Vanderbilt;began to collect for his wife soon after their marriage. This now comprises the first loop, one design to be close to the throat, the second and third coils being long enough to hang down over the bodice of a dress, the whole string going three times around the neck.

These larger pearls are without equals in the world. and meAoure fully a half-inch in diameter. There are about 50 of them, and a conservative valuation has recently fixed them worth at from 15,000 dole, to 20,000 dots each .making the group cost nearfv 1,000,000 dots. Many years were occupied in the search for these beauti- ful gems, and Europe and the Orient were ransacked in an endeavour to procure the finest pearls in the world. Many of these larger pearls have also an historic interest apart from their great purity and value, being the choicest specimens of sweral great collections, the pride of many a harem, the despair of many an owner, who only parted with these treasures through necossity or greed. These pearls will undoubtedly make a sensation when worn abroad by the young Duchess of Marlborough whose graceful throat seems just designed to be so adorned. As they pass into the keeping of the Marl- boroughs these pearls may become an heirloom in that family, to be passed on from one generation to another, until the great string is onca more broken up and dis- persed, and some other millionaire sets out to make another collection for his wife.

THE HIDDEN HAND;

CAPITOLA TIIE MADCAP. BY Mits. E. D. E. N. SOUTHWORTH.

CHAPTER XVII. ANOTHER STORM AT HURRICANE HALL.

"At this, Sir Knight grew high in wroth, And lifting hands and eyes up both, Three times he smote on stomach stout, From whence, at length, fierce words broke out." —iIUDIBRA2I.

The moon was shining full upon the river and the household beyond, when Capitola dashed into the water, and amid the sparkling and the leaping of the foam, made her way to the other bank, and rode up the rugged ascent. On the outer side of the lawn wall, the moonbeams fell full upon the little figure of Pitapat, waiting there. "Why, Patty, what takes you out so late as this?" asked Capitola, as she rode up to the gate. "Oh, Miss Caterpillar, I 'se waitin' for you! Ole liaise is dreadful, he is! jes fit to burst the shingles offen the roof with swearing! So I come out to warn you, so you can steal in the back way and go to yeur rooms so he won't see you, and I'll go and send Wool to put your horse away, and then I 'll bring you up some supper, and tell Ole Marse lbow you've been home ever so long, and gone to bed with a werry bad headache." " Thank you, Patty. It is perfectly astonishing, bow easy lying is to you. You really deserve to have been born in Rag Alley. But I won't trouble the Recording Angel to make another entry against you on my account." "Yes, Miss," said Pitapat, who thought that her mistress was complimenting her. "And now, Patty, stand out of my way. Una going to ride straight up to the horse-block, dis- mount, and walk right into the presence of Major Warfield 1" said Capitola, passing through the gate. "Oh, Miss Caterpillar, don't don't! he'll kill you, so he will 1" "Who 's afeared?" muttered Cap to herself, as she put her horse to his mettle, and rode gaily through the evergreens, up to the horse-block, where she sprang down lightly from her saddle. Gathering up her train with one hand and tossing back her head, she swept along toward the house with the air of a young princess. There was a vision calculated to test her firmness. Reader did you ever see see a raging lion tearing to and fro the narrow limits of his cage, and occa- sionally shaking the arrpilfitheatre with his tremend- ous roar? or a furious 'bull tossing his head or tail, and ploughing up the earth with his hoofs as he careered back and forth between the boundaries of his pen If you have seen and noted these mad brutes, you may form some faint idea of the frenzy of Old Hurricane, as he stormed up and down the floor of the front pi-,zza. Cap had just escaped an actual danger of too terrble a character to be frightened now by sound and fury. Composedly she walked up into the porch, and said: " Good evening, uncle." The old man stopped short in his furious strides, and glared upon her with his terrible eyes. Cap stood fire without bienching, merely remark- ing: "Now I have no doubt that in the days when you went battleing, that look used to strike terror into the heart of the enemy, but it doesn't into mine, somehow !" _ _ .

. _ " !" roared the old man, bringing down his cane with a resounding thump upon the floor; "Miss! how dare you have the impudenca to face me, much less the the—the assurance I—the effrontery I—the audacity! the brass to speak to me !" "Weil, I declare," said Cap, calmly untying her hat, "this is the first time I ever heard it was impudent in a little girl to give her uncle good evening." The old man trotted up and down the piazza two or three turns, then stopping short before the delinquent, he struck his cane down upon the floor with a ringing stroke, and thundered: " YOUNG WOMAN I tell me instantly, and without prevarication, where you have been ? " "Certainly, sir; 'going to and fro in the earth, and walking up and down in it! " said Cap, quietly. "FLAMES AND FURIES, that is no answer at all! Where have you been ? " roared Old Hurricane, shaking with excitement. "Look here, uncle. if you go on that way you'll have a fit presently 1" said Ca!:, calmly. "WHERE HAVE YOIT DEENA " thundered Old Hurricane. "Well, since you will know—just across the river, and through the woods and back again 1" "And eidn't 1 Forbid you to do that, minion? and how dare you disobey me? You, the creature of my bounty ! you the miserable little vagrant that I picked up in the alleys of New York, and tried to make a young lady of; but an old proverb says—'You can't make a salken purse out of a pig's ear 1 ' How dare you, you little beggar, disobey your benefactor I—a man of my age, character and poi Lion " Old Hurricane turned abruptly, and raged up and down the piazza. At this time Capitola had been standing quietly, holding up her train with one hand and her riding hat in the other. At this last insult she raised her dark grey eyes to his face with one long, indignant, sorrowful gaze, then turning silently away, and entering the house, she left Old Hurricane to storm up and down the piazza until he had raged himself to rest.

Reader! I do not defend, far less approve, poor Cap I I can tell her story and describe her as I have seen her, leaving her to your chaiitable interpretat:on. Next morning Capitola came down into the breakfast-room with one idea promiilent in her hard, little head—to which she ineutall7 gave expression: "Well as I like that old man, he must not permit himself to talk to me in that indecent strain, and so he must be made to know."

When she entered the breakfast-room, she found Mrs. Condiment already at tha kead or the table, and old Hurricane at the foot. He had quite got over his rage, and turned around blandly to welcome his ward, saying: "Good-morning, Cap." Without taking the slightest notice of the salu- tation, Cap sailed on to her seat. "Hump'n I did you hear me say, Good-morning,' Cap?" Without paying the least attention, Capitola reached out her hand and took a cup of coffee from Mrs. Condiment. "Humph! Humph! GOOD-MORNING, Capitola 1" said old Hurricane, widi marked emphasis. Apparently without hearing him, Cap helped her- self to a buckwheat-cake, and daintily buttered it. "Humph I humph humph! well, as you said yourself, a dumb devil, is better than a speaking one 11" ejaculated Old Hurricane, as he sat down and subsided into silence. Doubtless the old man would have flown into another passion, had that been possible; but, in truth, he had spent so much vitality in rage number one, that he had none left to sustain rage number two. Besides, he knew it would be necessary to blow up Bill Ezy, his lazy overseer, before night, and perhaps saved himself for that performance. He finished his meal in silence, and went out. Cap finished hors; and, "tempering justice with mercy," went up stairs to his room, and looked over all his appointments and belongings to find what she could do for his extra comfort; and found a job in newly-lining his warm slippers, and the sleeves of his dressing-gown. They met again at the dinner-table. "How do you do, Cap 1" said Old Hurricane, as he took his seat. Capitola poured out a glass of water and drank it In silence, and without looking at him. 'Oh! very well! a dumb devil, &c.,'" exclaimed Old Hurricane, addressing himself to his dinner. When the meal was over they again separated. The old man went to his study to examine his farm books, and Capitola back to her chamber to finish lining his warm slippers. Again at tea they met. "Well, Cap, is the dumb devil' cast out yet 2" be said, sitting down. Capitola took a cup of tea from Mrs. Condiment and passed it on to him in silence. "Humph, not gone yet, eh ?—poor girl! how it must try you 1" said Old Hurricane. After supper the old man found his dressing-gown and slippers before the fire all ready for his use. "Cap, you monkey! you did this," he said, turning around. But Capitola had already left the room. Next morning at breakfast there was a repetition of the same scene. Early in the forenoon Major Warfield ordered his horses, and, attended by Wool, rode up to Tip-Top. He did not return either to dinner or tea, but as that circumstance was not unusual it gave no uneasiness. MIL Condiment kept his supper warm, and Capitola had his dressing- gown and slippers ready. She was turning them before the fire when the iga mau arrived, He ..cape 42,ulte gaily. sad= 11,

"Now, Cap, I think I haVe fotincf-a (cr,lisnian- at last to cast out that 'dumb devil.' I heard you wishing for a watch the other day. Now, as devils belong to eternity, and have no business with time, of course the sight of this little time-keeper must put yours to flight!" and Po saying he laid upon the table, before the eyes of Capitola, a beautiful little gold watah and chain. She glanced at it, as it lay glittering and sparkling in the lamp-light, and then turned abruptly and walked away. " Humph ! that's always the way the devils do ! fly when they can't stand shot." Capitola deliberately walked back, laid a paper over the little watch and chain, as if to cover its fascinating sparkle and glitter, and said: "Uncle, your bounty is large, and your present Is beautiful; but there is something that poor Capitola values more than that--" She paused, dropped her head upon her bosom, a sudden blu-h fl -trued up over her face, and tear drops glistened in her downcast, eyes; she put both hands before her burning face for a moment, and then dropping them, resumed: "Uncle 1 you rescued me from misery, and per- haps, perhaps early death ! you have heaped benefits and bounties upon me without measure 1 you have placed me in a home a abundance, honour, and security I for all this, if I were mot grateful, I should deserve no less than death 1 But, uncle, there is a sin that is worse, or at least more ungenerous, than ingratitude 1 it is to put a helpless fellow creature under heavy obligations, and then treat that grateful creature with undeserved con- tempt and cruel unkindness 1" Once more her voice was choked with feeling. For some reason or other, Capitola's tears, per- haps because they were so rare, always moved Old Hurricane to his heart's centre; going towards tier softly he said: "Now, my dear, now my child, now my little Cap, you know it was all for your own good 1 Why, my dear, I never for one instant regretted bringing you to the house, and I wouldn't part with you for a kingdom Come now, my child, come to the heart of your old uncle." Now the soul of Capitola naturally abhorred senti- ment! If ever she gave way to serious emotion, she was sure to avenge herself by being more capricious than before. Consequently flinging herself out of the caressing arms ot Old Hurricane, she exclaimed:

"Uncle! I won't be treated with both kicks and halfpennies by the same person—and so I tell you. I'm not a cur to .be fed with roast-beef and beaten with a stick I nor, nor, nor a Turk's slave to be caressed and oppressed as her master likes I—Such abuse as you heaped upon me, I never heard—no, not even in Rag Alley." "Oh, my dear, my dear for Heaven's sake forget Rag Alley.'' "1 -'t!---- I vow I'll go back to Rag Alley, for a 1

won go 0h,.. very little more! Freedom and peace are even sweeter than wealth and honours 1" "Ah, but I wouldn't let you, my little Cap.' "Then I'd have you up before the nearest magis- trate, to show by what right you detained me 1 Ah, ha! I wasn't brought up in New York for nothing!" " Whee-ew and all this because, for her own good, I gave my own niece and ward a little gentle admonition." "Gentle admonition Do you call that gentle ad- monition ? Why, uncle, you are enough to frighten most people to death with your fury! You are a perfect dragon! a griffin! a Russian bear! a Bengal tiger! a Numidian lion! I declare if I don't write and ask some menagerie man to send a party down here to catch you for his show. You'd draw, I tell you 1" "Yes! especially with you for a keeper to stir me up once in a while with a long pole I" "And that I'd engage to do—cheap 1" The entrance of Mrs. Condiment with the tea-tray put an end to the controversy. It was, as yet, a drawn battle. "And what about the watch, my little Cap?" "Take it back, uncle, if you please." "But they won't have it back 1 it has got your Initials engraved upon it—look here," said the old man, holding the watch to her eyes. "C. L. N. Those are not my initials," said Capitola, looking up with surprise. "Why, so they are not! the blamed fools have made a-mistake I—but you'll have to take it, Cap." "No, uncle, keep it for the present," said Capitola, who was too honest to take a gift that she felt she did not deserve, and yet too proud to confess as much.

_ _ Peace was proclaimed—for the present. Alas! 'twas but of short continuance. During these two days of coolness and enforced quietude Old Hurricane had gathered a store of bad humours that required expenditure. So the very next day something went wrong upon the farm, and Old Hurricane came storming home, driving his overseer, poor, old, meek Billy Ezy and his man Wool, before him. Billy Ezy was whimpering; Wool was sobbing aloud; Old Hurricane was roaring at them both as he drove them on before him—swearing Ezy should go and find himself a new home, and Wool should go seek another master. And for this cause Old Hurricane was driving them on to his study, that he might pay the over- seer his last quarter's salary, and give the servant a written order to find a master. He raged past Capitola in the hall, and meeting Mrs. Condiment at the study door, ordered her to bring in her account book directly, for that he would not be imposed upon any longer, but meant to drive all the lazy, idle, dishonest eye-servants and time-servers from the house and land! "What's the matter now 2" said Capitola meet- ing her. "Oh, child, he's in his terrible tantrums again He gets jut o these ways every once in a while, when a young calf perishes, or a sheep is stolen, or any- thing else goes amiss, and then he abuses us all for a pack of loiterers, sluggards and thieves, and pays us off and orders us off. We don't go, of course, because we know he doesn't mean it; still, it is very trying to be talked to so. Oh! I should go, but, Lord, child! he's a bear, but we love him." Just as she spoke the study door opened, and Bill liCzy came out sobbing, and Wool, lifting up his voice and fairly roaring. Mrs. Condiment stepped out of the parlour door. "What's the matter, you blockhead 7" she asked of Wool. "0! 800-hoo-woo I Ole Marse been and done end gone and guy me a line to find an—an—another ....-Boe-hoo-woo!" sobbed Wool, ready to break his beart. "Give you a line to find another 800-boo-woo! I wouldn't do it if I were you, Wool 1" said Capitola. "Give me the paper, Wool," said Mrs. Condi- ment, taking the " permit " and tearing it up, b.nd adding: "There! now you go home to your quarter, and keep out of your old master's sight until he gets over his anger, and then you know very well that it will be all right. There go along with you." Wool quickly got out of the way, and made room for the overseer, who was snivelling like a whipped school-boy, and to whom the housekeeper said: "I thought you were wiser than to take this so to heart, Mr. Ezy I " "Oh, mum! what could you expect 7—an old narvint as has sarved the major faithful these forty years, to be discharged at sixty-five I Oh! hoo-000-000 1" whimpered the overseer. "But then you have been discharged so often, you ought to be used to it by this time! you get discharged just as Wool gets sold—about once a month but do you ever go?" "Oh, mum! but he's in airnest this time 'deed he is, mnm terrible in airnest 1 and all about that misfortnet bob-tail 'colt getting stole! I know how it wur some of Black Donald's garg as done It! As If I could always be on my guard against they devils! And he means it this time. mum! He's terrible in airnest I " " Tut 1 he's always in earnest for as long as it lasts! Go home to you family and to-morrow go about your business, as usual." Here the study bell rang violently and Old Hurricane's voice was heard calling—" Mrs. Condi- ment! Mrs. Condiment!" "Oh, lor I he coming!" cried Bill Ezy, running off as fast as age and grief would let him. "Mrs. Condiment 1 Mrs. Condiment I" nailed the Voice. "Yes, sir I yes "answered the housekeeper, hurry- ing to obey the call. Capitola walked up and down the hall for half an hour, at the end of which Mrs. Condiment came out "with a smile on her lip and a tear in her eye," and saying: "Well, Miss Capitola, I'm paid off and discharged also!"

"What for? " "For aiding and abetting the rebels I in a word, for trying to comfort poor Ezy and Wool." "And are you going 7" " Certainly not! I sha'n't budge! 1 would not treat the old man so badly as to take him at his word 1" and, with a strange smile, Mrs. Condiment hurried away just in time to escape Old Hurricane, who came raving out of the study. "Get out cf my way, you beggar !" he cried, pushing past Oapitola, and hurrying from ths Wm.

"Well, I declare, that was pleasant!" thouglit Cap. as she entered the parlour. "Mrs. Condiment, what will he say when be comes back and finds you all here still. " she asked. "Say 7—nothing. After this passion is over, fat will be so exhausted that he will not be able to geo tap another rage in two or three days." "Where has he gone ?"

"To Tip-Top; and alone, too; he was so mad with poor Wool that he wouldn't even permit hiril to attend." "Alone 9 has he gone alone I Oh, won't I give him a dose when he comes back 7" thought Capitols.. Meanwhile Old Hurricane stormed along towardS Tip-Top, lashing off the poor dogs that wished to follow him, and cutting at every living thing that crossed his path. His business at the village was to get bills printed and posted, offering an additional reward for the apprehension of "the marauding outlaw Black Donald." That day, he dined at the village tavern—" The Antlers," by Mr. Merry—and differed, disputed, or quarrelled, as the case might be, with every man whom he happened to come in contact with.

Towards evening he set off for home. It was much later than his usual hour of returning; but he felt weary, exhausted and indisposed to come into his own dwelling where his furious temper had created so much unhappiness. Thus, though it was very late, he did not hurry; he almost hoped that every one might be in bed when he should return. The moon was shining brightly when he passed the gate and rode up the evergreen avenue to the horse- block in front of the house. There he dismounted and walked up into the piazza, where a novel vision met his surprised gaze. It was C4pitola, walking up and down the floor, with rapid, almost masculine strides, and apparently in a state of great excitement. "Oh, is it you, my little Cap? Good evening, my dear," he said, very kindly. Capitola "pulled up" in her striding walk, wheeled around, faced him, drew up her form, folded her arms, threw back her head, set her teeth, and glared at him. " What the demon do you mean by that " cried Old Hurricane. _ _ _ _ _

" SIR " she exclaimed, bring down one foot with a sharp stamp—" SIR, how dare you have the impudence to face me, much less the—the—the— the brass the bronze! the coPru to speak to me ? " "Why, what in the name of all the lunatics ID Bedlam dees the girl mean 7 Is she crazy " exclaimed the old man, gazing upen her in astonish' ment. Capitola turned and strode furiously up and clown the piazza, and then, stopping suddenly and facing him, with a sharp stamp of her foot, exclaimed: "OLD GENTLEMAN, tell me instantly, and without prevarication, where have you been?" "To the demon with you! what do you mean? have you taken leave of your senses ? " demanded Old Hurricane. Capitola strode up and down the floor a few times, and stopping short and shaking her fiat, exclaimed:

"DIDN'T you know, you headstrong, recklese, desperate, frantic veteran didn't you know the jeopardy in which you placed yourself by riding out alone at this hour ? Suppose three or four great runaway negresses had sprung out of the bushes-- and—and—" She broke off, apparently for want of breath, and strode up and down the floor; then, pausing sud- denly before him, with a stern stamp of her foot and a fierce glare of her eye, she continued: "You shouldn't have come back here any more! No dishonoured old man should have entered the house of which I call myself the mistress!" "Oh, I take I take! ha! ha! ha! Good, Cap, good You are holding up the glass before me; but your mirror is not quite large enough to reflect 'Old Hurricane,' my dear—' I owe you one,' " said the old man, as he passed into the house, followed by his capricious favourite.

Chicago retains its oil reputation as the city where divorces can be more quickly obtained than anywhere else in the world. A fortnight ago five judges granted a hundred divorces in three hours there, an average of twenty divorces to each juege, and nine minutes te each divorce. There was evidently considerable truth in tbe cry of a railroad porter at the Chicago station: " Change here for all Western cities. Plenty of time to have your lunch or get divorced before the train starts ! "

A FAMILY SKELETON.

" Consumption in the family "has been a menace tp countless hordes of people. It hangs over familes like a black, threatening cloud, casting gloom all about, and sometimes persuading people they have the disease when they haven't. And yet it is doubtful if Consump- tion is inherited. Some of our very highest authorities say it is not.

Mrs. M. B. Irons, I, Northgaie, Huddersfield, hit the nail on the head when she wrote : " my children, three little boys, were all born with a tendency to weak lungs."

This weak tendency is what children of Consumptive parents inherit. But they are no more unfortunate than the majority of other children in the world inherit a weak tendency of some sort, and urless they get their vitality up to normal, they are as likely to have Con- sumption as the children of Consumptive parents. The truth is, any form of wasting disease is likely to take possession ot a weak constitution, whether the victim be baby, child, or adult. Read what Mrs. Irons says : "1, NORTHGATE, HUDDERSFIELD, "Sept. 25th, 1895.

"Messrs. SCOTT & BORNE. Ltd., "47, Farringdon Street, London, E.C.

"GENTLEMEN,-It has been my misfortune to have Consumption in my family, and my children, three little boys, were all born with a tendency to weak lungs. I have already used Scott's Emulsion as a family remedy, and have found that it meets the requirements of a family like mine perfectly. "The first time that I used Scott's Emulsion was in Port Elizabeth, South Africa. My husband was then suffering from Hemorrhage of the Lungs. and Scott's Emulsion proved very valuable in his case, easing the cough almost immediately. In case of his taking cold, he took it for months together, and received great bene- fit from it.

"My second son was born in Port Elizabeth, and almost immediately after his birth I took the fever. My baby was taken away from me, and although he had the best of nurses and the best of care, he never made any progress. At six months old he was a poor, puny baby, and at this time we commenced to give him Scott's Emulsion. We found that the first bottle did him a great deal of good. He was much stronger and healthier and showed more vitality in every way. We used to rub Scott's Emulsion into his chest, and we found that this did him good also. I can say that all of my children have taken Scott's Emulsion, and I think that it has been the means of saving their lives and making them healthy and strong as they are at present. Scott's Emulsion have been the means of overcoming their natural weakness of lungs. They do not now show any of the wasting tendencies which were manifes t when they were babies. "I have had many opportunities of observing the effects of Scott's Emulsion, and I can truly say that it is a remarkable curative agent for Throat and Lung com- plaints. It overcomes the emaciation always present in lung diseases, and cures the local inflammation of the lungs. For weak chests, coughs or colds, or any weak- ness or want of vitality, it is invaluable. I take it always when I feel" run down," and it has never failed me, either in personal use or for my children. It is a wonderful remedy. "Yours very truly, M. B. IRONS."

SIR J. T. BRUNNER, M.P., ON THE ARMENIAN QUESTION.

On Monday night the members of the Chester Liberal party held a banquet, in celebration of Mr. Gladstone's birthday, at the Chester and Counties Liberal Club, Watergate-street, Chester. Sir John Brunner, M.P., amidst loud applause, proposed the health of "The Right Hon. W. E. Gladstone." Referring to the chair- man's remarks that he (Sir John Brunner) was the only Liberal representative of Cheshire, he said he was pain- fully aware of the solitary gradeur of his position in the county, but he hoped that the gathering that night was an earnest augury of better things to come for the Liberal party. (Applause.) He mentioned that their object was to commemorate the birthday of the greatest leader the Liberal party had ever bad. Ile hoped they would send a hearty message of their good wishes that his health might be preserved, and that he might Eve a happy life, the one thing that a man of his age needed in this world. (Applause.) He (Sir John Brunner) was a grandfather, and he knew that when a man became one his main hope was in the happiness of his family (Applause.) They spoke of Mr. Gladstone as their leader, and there was no doubt that though be was re- tired from office and living a family life he was still their leader. (Loud applause.) To him the whole world had looked and not only the party fer advice and guidance during the past few weeks. It was the most touching incident of Mr. Gladstone'a position and it was most gratifying to those who had always been his loyal followers; it was the strongest proof to all those who had decried him that he was not only the leader of the Liberal party but the leader of humanity throughout the ' world. (Loud applause.) He would say in the name of those present, and on his own behalf, that he cordially welcomed the Duke of Westminster back as a follower of Mr. Gladstone—(loud applause)—for what the duke had done in connection with the Armenian question en- titled him to their gratitude and respect. That morning there had been a couple of letters in the newspapers on the subject, one from Mr. Gladstone and the other from , Lord Rosebery. The latters's letter did his heart credit, and he (Sir John Brunner) was cordially pleased to see it. hut he regretted to say that be did not follow Lord Rosebery to the end of his letter. He did not arrive with the same satisfaction at the conclusion that it was his duty at present to be silent, and to wait for the opening of Parliament. II would ask to be allowed to lay before Lord Rosebery one aspect of this great question which had not yet been publicly pressed upon the attention of the people of this country. He meant that oar Govern- ment had not published official or consular reports from Armenia during the past three and a half years. During the session from January to June last Mr. Francis Step- henson and a few colleagues pressed the Government to publish these reports,but the request was never acceded to. He would ask Lord Rosebery, in spite of the fact that the Government had refused to produce these re- ports, to press upon the Government, and especially up- on Lord Salisbury as Prime Minister and Foreign Secre- tary, that those reports should be published. He asked that Lord Rosebery should go a step further and ask that the Government should invite all the Governments hav- ing consular representatives in Asia Minor to publish the reports which had been received from those agents. Lord Rosebery told them that it might be the Govern- ment was facing the alternative of abandon- ing the Armenians or enduring an European war. He (Sir John Brunner) could not accept that alternative until our Government had made an effort, and had done its own duty in publishing its consular reports, and made the appeal to the civilised Governments of the world. Not until the Goveinment of Great Britain bad appealed to the Government of the world, and through them to the people of Christendom, and to all humanity could he allow that they were facing the alternative of an European war. He was one of those who were not profound believers in secret diplomacy or for the necessity of absolute secrecy in matters of foreign policy. They had had an excellent lesson in what had occurred between America and England within the last fortnight. We had been our own foreign ministers. As to whether there should be war or not he was satisfied that if the people of Great Britain were appealed to there would be no fear of war in Europe, but; there would be an unanimous demand from our countrymen that the Armenians should be saved, no matter what the risk might be. (Loud applause.) He commended the Bishop of Chester for the message he had forwarded to New York, for it was one of the most eloquent and earnest telegrams which had been sent from England. Con- cluding, Sir John Brunner said they all wished Mr. Gladstone health, a happy family life,and that to the end he might preserve his bright, his brave, and his noble spirit. They accorded Mr. Gladstone their veneration, and sent him their love. (Loud and continued applause.) The toast was drunk amid enthusiasm, and three hearty cheers were given for Mr. Gladstone.

Late on Saturday night a huge warehouse in Claypit- lane, Leeds, occupied by Messrs. Hepworth and Co wholesale clothiers, was found to be on fire. A stron body of firemen strove to distinguish the flames, but had eventually to allow the building to go while they tried to save the adjoinine property. In spite of their efforts, a volunteer drill hall and a house took fire, but these minor outbreaks were soon overcome. Messrs. Hepworth's place was totally destroyed, and damage es- timated at £60,000 was done. About ? tbousand people will be thrown out of employment. A severe snowstorm, the heaviest of the season,passeti over Central and Western Perthshire on Saturday. In the evening snow-ploughs had to be used to render vehicular traffic practicable.

SATURDAY EVENING CONCERT IN PARR HALL.

Another off the series of concerts for the people was given in Parr Hall, Warrington, on Saturday even- ing, but the attendance was again only moderate. It has been remarked on previous occasions that the plat- form of the:Hall looked depressingly bare when not used for orchestral purposes, therefore, the provision of a large number of plants and shrubs from the Town Hall, proved a much appreciated piece of thoughtfulness. The artistes looked considerably less isolated and the effect was also a relief to the eye. A lengthy programme had been compiled, while the artistes engaged were each and all of superior merit, and included Miss Marie Burnett (soprano, of the London and Glasgow Concerts), Miss May Wayman (contralto, of the Manchester, Liverpool, and Provincial Concerts), Mr. George Barton (the Lan- cashire tenor), Mr. Eaton Batty (baritone, of the Lon- den and Provincial Concerts), Mr.F.Weston (solo violon- cello: Sir Charles Hallii's and De Jong's Orchestra, &c.), Mr. G. Theakstone, (humorist), and Mr. F. Moss accompanist. All acquitted themselves admirably, and it is difficult to make any selection, though perhaps Miss Wayman was the more popular of the two ladies. Mr. Theakstone, as humorist, was a great success, and his "Laughing Song" (original), surpassed considerably the many that have been given in Warrington of late. "He went to a Party,"" I'm so Unhappy," and "The Dandy Coloured Coon." were also sung by him in first- class style. Mr. Frank Weston played up to his reputation, and his selections " Spanischer Tana" (Popper). " Nocturne " (Chopin) and "Dense Hollan- daise" (Dunkley), were all interpreted with fine expres- sion and skill, the latter being encored. Miss Wayman's voice appeared specially suited to the music of the favourite songs of "The Holy City" and "Killarney," and her renditions of them met with the warmth of reception they merited. Mr. Eaton Batty possesses a very powerful voice and "Comrades to Anna" and "The Naval Brigade" were sung by him in a manner both vigorous and masterly. There was also no mistak- ing tbe fact that Mr. George Barton understood to the full the capabilities of a tenor voice and the audience would not be denied an encore for his first song "The Amhor's Weighed," while "The Bay of Biscay" in his hands proved almost as captivating. In addition to the solos there was the part-song " I love my love in the morning," in which Miss Wayman, Miss Burnett. Mr. Barton and Mr. Batty all assisted; the duet" Prithee, Pretty Maiden" was rendered by Miss Burnett and 1111 r. Batty in a meritorious manner; " Home to our Moun- tains" from " Trovatore" was a treat, the voices of Miss Wayman and Mr. Barton mingling harmoniously. The programme concluded with the q•lartette "Good Night Beloved," in which the same four artistes participated. During the whole evening the items on the programme sustained the interest and enjoyment of the audience, and the concert was in every way one of the best miszel- aneous concerts given in Warrington since the Parr Hall opened. Judged an its merits the whole entertainment was worthy of "a crowded house," and the people of Warrington by not attending in larger numbers to these concerts are missing fan excellent means of recreation in the shape of good music at a cheap rate.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

Warrington are still unable t) beat Oldham, and wi'l now have to wait for another season before they yet another chance to beat the team which represents Spindledom. This team is the one team which of late years has always proved too much for Warrington, and do what they will Warrington cannot lick them. For four consecutive seasons, Oldham have had the better of Warrington, for daring the time they have won every match except two and those were draws. This year Oldham have won both fixtures, though the total num- ber ot points is less than in previous years. Last year Oldham won the first match by 29 points to three, and the second was a draw of six points each, making a total of 35 points to nine. This year the total is 9 points to nil. Of course victory was hardly expected on Saturday as six of the usual players were absent from the Warrington team.

It is astonishing how bad weather dogs the Warring- tonians whenever they go to Oldham. Last year was the only fine day they have ever spent there. Previous to then, the matches usually had to be cancelled or else fought under circumstances the reverse of pleasant. Although the weather fiend was kind on that occasion, the return match at Warrington had to be postponed on account of the frost. This year the weather was simply fearful at Oldham, and the players were soaked through before the match began.

The play itself, what there was of it, was chiefly in favour of Warrington. and it was more by luck than anything else that Oldham scored their one try. War- rington had plenty of bard lines, if any comfort is to be got out of this, and ought to have scored on several occasions. Burrows in particular had very bad luck, for he was only robbed after a grand dribble, just as be was on the point of scoring. It is getting time that War- rington's luck began to turn for they almost invariably play a good game, but are never favoured by fortune.

It was forward chiefly that Warrington were superior to Oldham. The Warrington forwards were much smarter than their opponents in every way, and it is a good sign that they have been improving every week. Burrows was the best of the lot, his dribbling being a treat to witness. Nevins. Barrett, and Wilcock were not far behind. Had the full strength of the Warrington forwards been there, the Oldhatners would have been made to" it up."

Varley and A. Lees were not up to their usual form on Saturday, and did nothing of any note. The bulk of the half back work on the Warrington side fell to Houghton, Clark having comparatively little to do. Houghton was in form on Saturday, and played the correct game under the circumstances, that of kick and rush, and he was very smart indeed at this. He was accused very much by the Oldham players of playing off side but be appar- ently kept just on side, for the referee appeared to be satisfied. ---

The threequarters were at a discount owing to the weather. Passing was completely out of the question, and neither side did anythinl worthy of note. Lawless was about the best of the Warrington backs,though there was very little in them. Scott was not a success at full back, and it was a mistake to put an untried man in that responsible position. He is a very good player in his right place of centre threequarters, but he has had no experience of full back play, and, therefore, cannot be held responsible for the mistakes he made. He,how- ever, made one really clever save. How he would have shaped had he had much to do, it would be diffi cult re say. He had as it happened comparatively little to do. It was a really good performance of a crippled team like Warrington to go to Oldham and only get beaten by a try. Had the full strength been on, a vic- tory would most likely have resulted.

I'ENKETH.

TEA MEETING AND ENTERTAINMENT.—On Tuesday evening the annual tea party and entertainment was held in the Wesleyan school. The tea was on the table at six o'clock and was partaken of by a large number of the congreaation and friends. The catering was in the hands of Mr. Atherton's Sunday school class, while a number of the young men officiated at waiters. After the tea was over, all adjourned to enjoy the excellent concert provided for them. The chair was taken by Mrs. R. Garnett, and, after a hymn had been sang, Mr. Garnett offered up a short prayer. After an interesting address by Mrs. Garnett relating to the institution of the annual tea party and watch-night service, and a refer- ence to the distressed Armenians, Miss Hilda Garnett gave a song entitled " Meditation," which was well applauded. Mr. R. J. Cooke then followed with a recitation entitled " Warriors of the Sea," which won well deserved applause. A quartet was next on the programme entitled " God is a Spirit," and was excellently sang by Mrs. Clarke, Miss M. Routledge, Mr. Joseph Smith and Mr. W. T. Wilkinson. Garnett then addressed the audience at some length or the penny ' bank and gave a short account of the workings of the institution, together with a summary of the past year's tramsactions. A song by Mrs. Clarke entitled "Far away," was warmly applauded and was followed by a duet by. Mrs. Withinshaw and Miss Hilda Garnett, the accom- panist being Miss F. Watson. The proceedings then term- inated with a closing hymm.

HOUSES OF SNOW IN ALASKA.

The natives of Alaska construct snow huts in about the time that would be required to pitch an ordinary wall tent. They select a place where the snow is about four feet deep. A space six feet by nine is marked out. Blocks two feet square are cut from the surface snow and set up on edge around the excavation for side walls. At one end three feet of the space is dug down to the ground ; in the balance about two feet of snow is left fora couch. The sides and ends ate built up tight, and the whole is roofed with broad slaps of crusted snow cut in proper dimensions to form a flat gable roof, and loose snow is thrown over all to chink in. At the end,which is dug down to the ground, a hale is cut large enough to admit a man crawling in on his hands and knees. The hut is now finished, and the bedding and provisions are packed inside. The arms and ammunition are generally left outside. After the outside work is finished everybody crawls into the hut, and the opening is stopped up from the inside with a plug of snow which has been fitted carefully,and no one is expected to go out until it is expected to break camp. The cembined heat from the bodies of the inmates, to- gether with the lamp they use, soon raises the tempera- ture, and a degree of comfort is obtained, no matter how cold it may be outside. When fuel is obtainable a kitchen is added to the structure,with a fireplace cut out of solid walls of snow. Fire in such a fireplace has been used for an hour a day for a month. The first heat softens the exposed snow, but the snow afterwards ehanges in solid ice,and remains uachanged so long as the temperature in the open air remains below zero. About the middle of April these snow houses are no longer available, as they become too damp for comfort, and the usual practice is to dig a hole down into the ground and roof the abode with skins.

===== 0002601_18960104_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
HAND AND BRAIN.

The actual amount of dexterity in the human hand has—says "Scieace Siftings," been measured with more or less accuracy, and its value in mechanical employ- ments traced from youth to age. Flow the hand grows old, gradually losing its skill, has been described by Sir James Crichton Bmwne, who has made a long course of investigations in rural towns. The high period of skill and endurance, this authority says, is from thirty to forty, the hand after that beginning to lose its muscular delicacies and its suppleness. Between the ages of seven- teen and eighteen, the hand of a boy grows into the hand of a man and first becomes valuable from a com- mercial point of view. If a workman is temperate and industrious, and continues to improve in his trade, his hand's dexterity increases until he is thirty. After forty the muscles do not respond nearly as readily and cer- tainly to the orders of the brain, and the quality and quantity of the work done begin to fall off. While a man in exceptionally fine health, and one especially , dexterous can often keep up his degree of skill long past the age of forty, such a man is an excep- tion. This comparatively early aging of the hand is an interesting and remarkable fact, as it is after forty, as a rule, that a carefully-used brain becomes the most valuable. Practically, none of our statesmen of the highest rank are under forty ; most of them are alsove fifty, and often ten years older than that. In the trades, on the other hand,the highest paid workmen, with hardly an exception, are under the lage of two score. The scale of wages in the button trade, for example, is a good indication of this tendency of the hand to grow old so early in life. At his very lbest,in his prime,a skilful button-maker can make 6,240 ivory buttons a day on his lathe. For this he receives 453. a week. At forty-five years of age it is only the exceptional man who can make more than 28s. a week. When the workman is sixty-five years of age he can seldom make more than 203., this providing that he still enjoys sound health. Of course,this is only the case in the trades where one hand is used continually and sys- tematically. A Sheffield knife-forger,for instance, strikes something like 28,000 blows with his hammer daily. An enormous amount of muscular and nervous force is 're- quired for this,and it is no wonder that the strain on the nerve centres and the muscles becomes visible in a few years. In farming or the seafaring life, or some other vocation in which the energy is more equally distributed over the entire body, the hand does not loseits cunning so early. Oftentimes, it retains its skill until the facul- ties generally commence to give way. It is the seden- tary occupation that tells, and the only remedy for it is such exercise as will divert the nerve current from the already overtaxed hands.

SHIP CANAL.

LAST WEEK'S TRAFFIC.

Last week was a comparatively quiet one on the canal, work being practically at a standstill on Wednesday and Thursday. The traffic consequently showed a marked falling off from the previous week's record, the arrivals and sailings numbering 100 against 136, and the aggre- gate net registered tonnage amounting to 31,454, against 47,000 tons during the preceding seven days. Coast- wise traffic suffered a diminution of 31 ships and 1,329 tons, and foreign traffic was less by four ships and 7,217 tons. Amongst the cargoes imported were 19 of general merchandise, nine of China clay, eight of (stone and slate, one of pit-clay, one of rock-salt, one of boulders, one of tinplate bars,three of pig iron,three of wood pulp and three of fruit. Twenty general cargoes were ex- ported, six of coal, one of salt, and one of pitch.

THE SHIP CANAL BILL.

By the Manchester Ship Canal Bill, which has been duly deposited for next session in the Private Bills Office of the House of Commons, power is sought to fill np the tidal openings in the parishes of Eastham, Frodsha.re, and Runcorn, which were constructed in the estuary of the Mersey in substitution for other openings, under powers given by the Manchester Ship Canal (Tidal Open- ings &c.) Act, 1890, The preamble of all the bill dealing with these powers says that " great inconvenience and expense have been .caused by the influx into the canal of silt and ice through the said tidal openings, and navi- gation is rendered difficult by the currents through these openings,and it is therefore expedient that the Company should be authorised to fill up the said tidal openings." In addition to these works, which will be carried out by levelling the embankments up to their normal height, the Company seek power to construct an embankment on the portion of Norton Marsh belonging to them, com- mencing on the northern side of the new road leading from Runcern to Warrington and terminating in the flood bank on tho easterly side of the Norton Marsh. The bill also provides for the construction of a railway two furlongs 8.14 chains in length, commencing on the westerly side of Trafford-road and terminating at the south-east corner of Dock No. 5. It is proposed to enact that the provisions contained in the Bridgewater Canal Rates Confirmation Act, 1894, for the recovery, of tolls and charges from by-traders on the Bridgewater canals," shall extend and apply, mutatis mutandis, to the recovery of all tolls, rates, and charges and all arrears thereof at any time owing to any person in re- spect of goods, merchandise, animals, or passengers con- veyed over any portion of any canal." It is further pro- posed to obtain power to utilise certain lands belonging to the Company at Walton Arches, situate between the embankment of the old railway of the London and North-Western Company and the deviation railway con- structed in pursuance of the Act of 1885, for the deposit of material dredged from the river Mersey and the canal. Power is sought to abandon the construction of the branch railway to the Arpley Meadow, which was originally authorised in 1885, and subsequently revived by the Act of 1894, after the time for its completion had expired in 1892. In the event of this abandonment being sanctioned, provision is made in the bill for the release of the deposit money, amounting to £1,223. It is proposed to amend the Act of 1885, which requires the Company to cause all the tidal waters of the river Weaver which flow into the canal to pass into the es- tuary of the river Mersey at each the, by the insertion of words requiring the Company to send the tidal water into the Mersey "during the ebb of the same tide." The Company seek to make additional by-laws for the Bridge- water canals, and power is proposed to be given to the Company to enclose" all or any of their canals, docks, sheds, warehouses, and buildings." The time granted by Parliament for the construction of Warrington Docks is proposed to be extended for !ten years from the Ist January, 1895, and it is proposed, finally, that the Com- pany and the Corporation of Warrington be authorised to enter into agreements " for the settlement of all or any questions pending between them."

QUEEN'S THEATRE OE VARIETIES.

The building so long known as Ohmy's Circus, has for the present changed its name and nature and has developed into a theatre of varieties or music-hall, and the lessees and proprietors are Messrs. Rogers and Greenall while Mr. T. Acton is general manager. A very presentable stage has been erected and the lighting and seating arrangements are satis- factory and in keeping with the nature of the place. The first opening of the theatre was on Monday when a programme of items of an attractive nature was submitted, consisting of ventriloquial sketches by Professor Grant, sentimental and comic songs, dancing, humorous dialogues, &c., by a collection of artistes whose names are familiar on the variety stage, and who included—Miss Alice Chasemore, Miss Theresa Roach, Messrs. G. Reynolds, J. C. Beffron, the Edlins, and Aug Babalo. All the performances are highly creditable and were received with great favour by the large attendances each evening. The last named artiste is a lightening depicter in chalks of statesmen well. known, and scenes of fair artistic excellence. Judging by the popularity and excellence of the first performances given, the new theatre has very fair chances of competing more than favourably with rival attractions of a similar nature.

Every now and then one hears of people loz..ing poi- soned from eating food preserved in tin cans. The amount of risk we incur from eating these preserved foods is exceedingly small, and it is likely to be done away with altogether, for paper cans are now being manufactured in the United States which are designed to supemde the use of those of tin altogether.

DARING BURCILAttY AT RUNCORN.

SMART CAPTURE.

At Runcorn Police Court, on Friday, before Messrs. F. J. Norman, chairman, and E. Imison, James ,Shaw, no fixed abode was charged with burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Joseph Wilding, on the night of the 26th December, with intent to steal. Joseph Wilding, a caretaker for Mr. Ashton, solicitor, said that early on the previous morning he awoke, and fancied he could smell wood burning. He lit a candle, came downstairs, and went into the magistrates' clerk's office, but saw nothing unusual. He then went into the lobby, and saw a light proceeding from the kitchen. He saw a man in a corner, and he was standing with his back towards another office door. Witness exclaimed " Oh," and prisoner replied " I have taken nothing." Witness then said" What are you doing here ? " and prisoner replied "It is all right," to which witness an- swered " It is not all right. What are you doing here ?' Witness said that the man had no boots nor coat on,and he could identify him in the dock as being the same per- son. Prisoner ran along the lobby through the kitchen into the yard, and out at the back. Witness shouted to his wife, and in consequence of what he said, the latter shouted through a window " Murder," and " Police." As a result, Dr. McDougall blew a policeman's whistle. Witness bolted the back door, and came to the front, where P.S. Brewster, in company with two other con- stables, met him. On examining the kitchen he found five drawers were ope.ned,as well as a cupboard.. Nothing was missing. When the sergeant came he found a hole in the roof of the storeroom. Two ladders were also found reared one against the scullery wall and the other against another wall. P.S. Brewster, in the course of his evidence, said the scullery window had been opened from the top. Under- neath the window in the yard he found a nair of clogs (produced), which he identified as being the same as those prisoner was then wearing. In consequence of find- ing the clogs he suspected prisoner, and directed P.C.'s Wylie and Booth to search in the Camden soap works, at the rear of the premises. Prisoner was apprehended by P.C.Wylie. When charged with the offence he said, " I don't know how I got there, but I found myself there. I took nothing, only a small piece of meat on a plate." P.C. Wylie said he was in company with the last wit- ness, and went with him to Mr. Ashton's office. By his direction lie went and searched in the Camden works, and found prisoner between two large soap boilers. He had the back of his coat turned over his head, endeav- ouring to hide himself.

Prisoner was now charged with burglariously entering and stealing from the dwelling-house, and he replied "I can only I was drunk,and I didn't know anything about it, until I found myself against the fire." In reply to the Magistrates' Clerk, P.S. Brewster said that prisoner was sober when brought to him at the offices.

The Chairman said that this was a mest serioug offence. Prisoner was an old offender, and would have to be sent to the Assizes for trial.
