Such was the subject of a celebrated sermon preached in Mamhester,but the more practical problem of the day is how to make life worth living on earth. One of the important tri- butaries of the river of happiness is the possession of a com- fortable home. A home made bright and cheertul not only by the kind and loving dispositions of those living there, but made attractive by the good taste displayed in the furnishing. Furnishing is a very important factor in the matter of con- tinued self-satisfaction. So much jerry new London furniture .is being now retailed by drapers that one needs to pause be- fore rushing impetuously into the first draper's because of a cheap rtd ticket. High-class makers, such as Lamb,Gillows, and Garnett,still exist, to whom the wealthy can still go with a long purse but to most people this would be a too expensive experiment. Again, the average man nowadays has a strong liking for a bargain ; anything obtainable at about half or quarter the cost has a strong fascination for him. Is it possi- ble to obtain such an advantage in furnishing? Yes, and more than possible, it is an absolute fact. We cannot do better than recommend a visit to MR. WORSLEY'S FUR- NISHING PANTHEON, GAYTHOUN, MANCHRSTRII.

frHE Successful Establishment of any of the ..large ware- ." houses of Manchester is always of interest to the citizens. One of the most brilliant examples of the value of perseverance, enterptise, and tact is the now busy hive of industry so well known as The Pantheon, which occupies such a prominent position in Gaythorn, near the Cmtral Station, Manchester. The building is a massive pile of 'modern design, 150 feet long, six storeys high, with show- room accommodation covering an area of over one million square feet. The plate-glass windows are each 12 feet square, each square if broken costing nearly £3O to replace. len of "these immense show windows form a portion of the frontage. This will give a faint idea of 'The Pantheon" in outward appearance, but ah !-here pen fails altegether in attempting to do justice to anything like an adequate description of the interior. "The Pantheon" was specially built to give the largest and most convenient floor space for showrooms, and cost £28,000. the floors specially constructed to carry enormous weight. To-day it stands as one of the smartest furnishing houses in England, containing by far the largest and most comprehensive stock of any two houses in this large city. From basement to roof, from floor to ceiling, "The Pantheon" fi with thousands of well bought, care- . to attempt description of which would futile as overflowingbargainLiverpool attempting 10 crush tune ll as l si eselectedd to

THE MANCHRSTRE SHIP CANAL.

The destinies of" The Pantheon" are c mtrolled and pre- sided over by its sole absolute proprietor. Mr. W. H. Worsley, whose sole study is an ever increasing desire to satisfy and please the thousands of customers who daily patronise and support his efforts. We invite the reader to accompany us in an imaginary visit to "The Pantheon " to see what it con- tains and how it is managed. On arrival we are at once stru2l; with the magnificent STARTLING APPEARANCE

of "The Great Pactheon," the immense long line of stupen- dous show windows displaying for sale capital specimens of high-class Second-Imnd Furniture oy high-class makers, the sale of which has made "The Pantheon famous.

UPON ENTERING THE BUILDING- we see at once that as far as stock is concerned there is sufficient furniture for nearly the whole of Manchester. We see at once visitors walking round inspecting from Olaham, Bolton. Bury, Burley, Bleckburn,Bacup. Rochdale, Preston. Warrington and all parts of Lancashire and Yorkshire, Cheshire, the Potteries, Wales, &c. It is the cardinal rule of " The Pantheon " to give the fullest facilities for inspec- tion without feeling compelled to buy. The ground floor is filled with the large specimens of bedroom furniture suitable for I.rge rooms; we are told the smaller suites are on the top floor, to which we can travel by means of the hoist, or walk. Bedroom suites on the ground floor we notice by Lamb, original cost £l5O, price £35 ; by Goodall, cost £lBO, for £5O; by Henry Ogden. cost £2OO, for £55. These are among the best suites, but one that strikes us with special attention is a fine hanosorne Bedroom Suite by Behan. in solid American walnut, heavily carved in has relief, with large wardrobe fitted with brilliant crystal bevel mirrors ; dressing table unique with neatly fitted jewel drawers, sur- mounted by large swing dressing glass ; washstand fitted with statuary marble and shaving mirror, th-ee chairs. pedestal table de nuit, and towel horse ; what originally cost £6O you can obtain for £25. Over 200 hedroom Suites are in stoJk, on show, from 355. to £lOO, forming a display so impressive that wears almost STRUCK DUMB WITH AMAZEMENT. We now go up stairs to the Dining-room Showroom. Wkat a sight, long avenues of Sideboards is the first impiession ; hundreds of Sideboards, with hundreds of Suites, also Dining Tables, Oyermahtels, splendid collection of Bronzes, Ormolu Clocks, Vases, &c. In this department we find magnificent Sideboards, original cost from £lOO to £2OO, at prices simply astounding, £lO to £2O. The next floor above is the Drawing- room Furniture Showroom, which is simply a show of

DAZZLING BEAUTY dissolving one's sensitive aptzeciation into impressive loveli- ness. No department is so interesting to la :lies as the draw— ing-room showroom; a keen delight is experienced in selecting weird and fantastic furniture for the drawing-room. /Esthetic, Moorish, and Egyptian Cabinets, peculiar Green and t,ronced Tables, Occasional Chairs of charming variety. Cosy Corners, Comfortable Lounging Settees and chAirs equal' to the highest class firms in the Kingdom at quarter the price. The largest showroom for Drawing-room kurniture in Manchester, containing over 100 Suites, 100 Cabinets, zoo' Overmantels, 500 Tables. Suites from £3 10s. Cd. to £5O. Cabinets from 30s. to £45. Marvellous value, worth in most cases three or four times the money charged. The next department adjoining is cases three or four times the money changed. The next de- partment is the Bedstead end Belding Department, which contains an enormous stock of Bedsteads. The brilliant- burnished brass displayed here is so immense as to preseYlt almost a

SEA OF GOLD. Magnificent specimens of the brasswnrkers' art is here die played to perfection. Brass Bedsteads may be purchased solid and complete for as low a price as 355. to the best at £35. A clear saving of 50 per cent. is effected in bedsteads and bedding. Brass and Iron Bedsteads, strong and durable, may be secured from 6s. Fd. to 30s. The bedding is all new, manufactured on the premises; everything is new in this department, the work here giving employment t:-) over 50' hands. Nothing is allowed to be used here except it has pre— viously been washed and purified. All bedding sold at Wor— bley's may be guaranteed as of SPOTLESS PURITY.

Wool Beds complete, guaranteed all wool. from Bs. 60., Hair Mattresses from 18s. 6d.. Feather Beds from 225. 6d. The next place of interest to visit is the Bedroom Furniture Department, which is displayed in a showroom, 63 yards by 25 yards. larger than most of the combined showrooms of most furnishing establishments. One is bound to admit that The Pantheon is certainly far ahead of any rival establish- ments in point of size and convenience. The showroom visited so far are lofty, well lighted, and, of immense area. The Bedroom Ferniture Showroom is magnificent; 300 Bedroom Suites are displayed in avenues, the finest collec- tion extant, at prices simply i . ncredible, astonishing every one. pulverising and exterminating all competition. Com- plete Bedroom suites, solid throughout, at 10s. up to £3O, magnificent in style and finish. Enamelled Suites from 30s. to £7. The next department is for Kitchen Furniture, the varied stock of which is quite sufficient to satisfy the most exacting. Thousands of chairs, hundreds of tables and dressers, every requisite for the kitchen at half the usual , prices charged. A department is also provided for Iron- mongery. But we must move on, our examination and in- spection is taking t,,ci long and occupying too much fqpi.m. We nuw are shown into the Carpet and Oilcloth Department, which contains thousands of new rolls of carpets, Brussels from is. Hid , Tapestry from led., Axminster from 2s. llid. and so on. Then we see hundreds of Second-hand Carpets. If you want a carpet to beat creation, buy one of Worsle3r's secondhand carpets ; they knock the competition of Jew and Gentile into smithereens. You can get a second-hand Brussels carpet, no tapestry ; no, real. genuine Brussels, from 4to 5 yards by 3to 4 yards, for under a sovereign. Hugs of im- mense variety, lovely Skin Rugs, Axminster, in facto! every conceivable design, shade, and colour, forming a gorgeous display.

RIVALLING THE RAINBOW.

Oilcloths and Linoleums are here cuts to pieces; thousands of yards are here daily slashed and cut intaall shapes to fit the various houses they are intended for. The prices are also cut, in fact, cut so fine that you would require a magnifying glass to discover any profit made out of this department. We saw Oilcloths from lid. per ya-d and Linoleums from 6& per yard. The best Scotch Oilcloths and Linoie ms are displayed from Is. 3d. per yard to 2s. 11d. We should like to 'peak of all the wonderful things displayed for sale, but we must be pre. paring to leave The Pantheon in order to c Itch our train. We, therefore, hu, ry throug" tho Offim Furniture Department, the Brass Fender and Firebrass Department. the Miscellane- ous Department quickly on to the latest addition, the Music Department. Hark! What do we hear?

MUSIC. HEAVENLY AND SUBLIMB. The "Hallelujah Chorus," Hividel's masterpiece, is being rolled along in glorious harmony by a gentleman who lates wc discovered to be " The Proprietor.' American Organ are here displayed in reat variety, mostly secondhand for. instance, a Mason and Hamelin organ os,u be purchased for 10 guineas which cost £4O. The iianofortea sic in strong evidence, and a great credit to Mr. Worsley, who buys everything for "The Pantheon" himself. Pianos by Erard, Steinway, Broadwoud, Bechstein, Justin Browne, Col- lard, Brinsmead, and all the best makers are displayed at prices simplY amazing. Brard pianos, the king of all In- struments. are priced at £l5, £2O, and Ma. Marvellous value. THE EX AMIN ER. SATURDAY, January 4th, 1896.

WUATEVER may be said of the The Foreign Outlook. prospects of home trade at the

beginning of another ;;;;;—;;;;;;; the whole the auguries seem favourable in tha t respect—the country can hardly be congratulated on the promising outlook in regard to its foreign relations. In America, in South Africa, and in both the Near and the Far East there are un- settled questions which may at any time develop critical situations. Since the traexpected scare of a fortnight ago there seems to be a tendency to belittle the importance of the now issues between Great Britian and the United States that were raised by the PRESIDENT'S message. Yet, as a matter of fact, the Venezuelan controversy stands Prettyimuch where it did on the day that Mr. CLEVELAND'S remarkable utterances were cabled across the Atlantic. The American Congress has adopted with practical unanimity the PRESIDENT'S dictatorial proposal to appoint a commission to inquire into the boundaries of a British colony. It may be further assumed that Congress also endorsed—it certainly did not protest against— the PRESIDENT'S recommendation that the findings of the commission, if they did not square with British ideas of equity, should be enforced by the material powers of the United States. That is the account as it stands between this country and the United States at the present time, and even the most optimist can hardly say that it is entirely free from the possibility of com- plications. The commission will, doubtless, pursue its inquiries more or less thoroughly and then it will report. What will be the nature of its report? It is clear that momentous results may depend upon the answer to this question. If the com- mission wishes to meddle with a frontier line which has been settled for many years past, and which is considered by this country to be as much out- side the scope of foreign arbitration as the boundary of an English county,then the situation will Inofaty v hbeeelcraohnmagpeusaextremely critical. Mr. CLEVELAND language eridhee huismedseilnf his message;brusqueness possibly think that that is the way a " Great Republic" should talk to the "effete Monarchic)

of Europe, but he may yet live to learn that in dealing with international affairs a little diplomatic reserve is not to be despised. In the event of an unfavourable 'report by the commission he has left his country practically no means of escape from a disastrous war except the humiliating pro- cess known as "climbing down" or persuading by peaceful means Great Britain to "climb down." On the whole the situation that has been created is not creditable to the statesmanship of the American President, and it may even yet require all the "common sense" that Mr. GLAD- STONE referred to in order to avoid a deplorable rupture between the two great branches of the Anglo-Saxon race. Another startling illustration of the enormous amount of inflammable material—politically speaking of course lying scattered about the British Empire has been given this week in South ' Africa. We refer to the unexpected and, it would seem, lawless invasion of the Transvaal by Dr. JAMESON with a force of eight hundred or a thousand fully armed men. Dr. JAMESON is an officer of the British South African Company and he and all the men under his command are British subjects. The Tranvaal is a Republic con- trolled by the Boers, descendants of the old Dutch settlers in South Africa, nnd apart from the fact that it acknowledges the suzerainty of the British Crown in regard to its foreign affairs, it is practi- &fly an independE.nt country. This inva- sion of a friendly country tan, therefore, so far as is yet known, only be described as one of the most audacious piratical raids known to history. The reasons or excuses that I will be made for Dr. JAMESON'S action are easily anticipated. The British residents in the: Transvaal have at the present time some serious grievances to complain of. They are superior to the Boers in numbers, in wealth and in education yet they are not &lowed any voice in the govern- ment of the country. They provide most of the taxes necessary for the administration of the state but they exercise no control over the spending of this money. And it is said that in addition to denying them the franchise their Boer rulers have shown an increasing disposition to harrass them in their business operations and to discourage further British immigration by various acts of petty tyranny, though it must be admitted that many of these allegations have been stoutly repudiated. On the other hand, it cannot be doubted that the Outlanders," as the new resid- ents of the Transvaal are described, have been a severe thorn in the side of the phlegma- tic Dutchmen. They have demanded the franchise with increasing persistency, and recently there have been open threats that they would secure their demands by fore, of arms if necessary. The Boers have not be( slow to declare that they will meet force with . force, and it is not doubted that they will be as good as their word. It is evident therefore that an attempt will be made to exonerate Dr. JAMESON on the ground that he advanced into the Trans- vaal for the purpose of protecting his own country- men from the violence of the Boers. That the British Government is not of opinion that this invasion of a friendly state was justifiable is shown by the peremptory commands that were sent out to Dr. JAMESON to immediately retire from the Transvaal. It is now known that the interfer- ence of the home government has not :been suc- cessful in preventing an armed conflict between the opposing sides, Dr. Jameson's rash act hav- ing ended in a disastrous defeat and a complete surrender of the whole of his forces. The rela- tions between the" Outlanders" and the Boers will now have to be definitely settled in one fashion or another, and, considering that both France and Germany claira to have interests in the Transvaal, a satisfactory settlement may not be easily arrived at.

According to the reports upon Home Trade. commercial matters which have been published within the last few days there has been an improvement in trade during the year that has just closed, though the improvement has not, on the whole, been of a very decided character. There was between the years 1890 and 1894 a fall in the value of the exports from this country to the extent of £13,500,000 ; last year the tide turned and a recovery was made to the value of £8,295,000. This would seem to show that the period of depression has come to an end and that a period of expansion has begun. More than half the increase is accounted for by the larger quantities of yarns and woven pods exported. Amongst the other manufactured or partly manufactured commodities for which foreign or colonial customers have sent larger orders are enumerated cycles, coal products, earthenware, leather, wool, arms and ammunition and machinery and millwork. It is just possible that JOHN BULL may regard the last-named articles with a certain amount of dubiety. An increased demand for arms and ammunition is, for instance, not a reassuring symptom, for these lethal weapons cannot be used in many parts of the world without disturbing the peace of one section or another of British subjects. Then, an increasing export of English-made machinery is not an altogether satisfactory :thing to contem- plate. Is this machinery to be used by intending competitors in the Far East and elsewhere to the detriment of British trade? That is a question which deserves the attention of both manufac- turers and artisans in this country. Last year will bo famous for the speculative mania over ISouth Africa mining schemes. So wild was this speculation that at one time the value of the shares of gold-mining companies with a nominal capital of £50,000,000 reached the stupendous Stock Exchange value of £250,0)0,000. The shares of one mine which were quoted at 14s. in September, 1894, were afterwards valued at fersot 12 12s. Gd., and in another case the rise was m othereilhal.aisb. ee6nd. a clotnaiidsernacbelde- Ia tosss.ay 6d h. to decline since the highest figures were reached • There was however no improvement in agriculture during last year, as an additional half 'million acres went out of arable cultivation. In the carry- ing industries, railways showed an increase of re- ceipts especially in the latter part of the year, but on the other hand 1895 is spoken of as one of the worst years, if not the worst year, on record for shipowners. Yet in spite of low frieghts there was an increase of about 700,000 tons in ship- ping during the year.

Some further particulars of the determined and business-like attempt to open up a direct traffic be- tween Manchester and the Australian ports have been given in the Manchester " Guardian.' Ai:- cording to an Australian correspondent of that jour- nal the deepest interest is telt at Aelborne and Sydney in the pioneer voyage of the steamer, Gulf of Siam, which is due in Manchester towards the end of January. Her cargo will be made up of 43,000 caresses of mutton, 6,000 bales of wool, 50 crates of rabbits, 100 tons of preserved fruits and meat, and various smaller quantities of antipodean

The news from Armenia does not improve as time goes on. A Constantinople correspondent of the "Speaker "gives fitting and graphic ac- count of the persecution of the Christians which still continues in spite of all protests by the Eng- lish and other Governments. This correspondent says" A number of villages in the Erzeroum, Van, and Kharput vilayets have accepted Mahometan- iim ; one bishop and a number of priests in differ- ent places have yielded under torture. The churches have been destroyed, and in several places the peo- ple burned alive in them. At Kharput one church has been changed into a mosque, another into a stable. The great monasteries have been sacked and burned. In a number of villages the people were stripped of their clothing and left naked, without food or shelter. From most of them every moveable thing was carried off and the houses des- troyed. Money sent from Constantinople to re- lieve some of the refugees has been seized by the authorities under pretence of collecting taxes from them. The Commissioners sent from here to carry out the promised reforms are treating the Armenians who have escaped the massacres as though they had no right to live, and there is a reign of terror in most of the towns where the mas- sacres have taken place. This, in brief, is the con- dition of the people whom England undertook to protect, for whom England, Russia and France de- manded security for person, property, and religion, and who are recommended to be patient by the six Great Powers. And the Sultan sits in his palace and laughs !" Still later reports confirm the allegation that even the relief sent out from England to save from hunger and starvation the Armenians who survive the numerous massacres is seized by the Turkish officials or their allies the Kurds. Yet in the face of all this,that wonderful " concert" of Europe upon which Lord Salisbury seemed to place so much re- liance can apparently do nothin .

The Warrington Dramatic Society is I con- gratulated upon the success of the dramatic per- formances recently given in the Royal Court Theatre. As a result of the three night's entertain- ment the Society has found itself in a position to hand over no less a sum than one hundred guineas in aid of the Infirmary. It is needless to say that this donation will be very acceptable to the honor- ary treasurer and the committee of management of the Infirmary. The wo of the Infirmary in- creases 80 continuously that it is now not an easy task to make the income cover the expenditure, and there is, we believe, reason to fear that unless the

sources of income are increased either by public or private generosity the institution will not be able to cope with the needs of a growing population at all costs not so thoroughly as in the past.

The Committee of the Warrington Floral and Horticultural Society were able to present a satisfactory report at the annual meeting which was held on Monday. They have been very succes lid with the shows which have been held during the past two years and they have therefore every en- couragement to go on with the society. It is a great pity that the gentry of the district do not show a little more sympathy with the society, for then its success would be assured beyond all ques- tion. Mr. John Crosfield has set them a very good example by his gift of bulbs and offer of prizes to those who made the best use of them. At the meeting of the society, a strong committee was ap- pointed, and with the assistance of the energetic secretary, and treasurer, Mr. Ryan, and Mr. J. Ful- ton Smith, another good season is I:Joked forward to.

AS9Atrt.TS ON TRig POUCH Al RUNCORN.

On Friday, at Runcorn Police Court, before Messrs. F. J. Norman and K. Imisun, John O'Hare Bacup, Lan- cashire, was charged with being drunk and disorderly, and also with assaulting P.C. Kendrick, whilst in the execution of his duty. The officer named stated that he was on duty in lialton-roadv on the 25th inst., about 9.15, and he saw the prisoner there. He wits stopping women and cursing them because they would not give him money. The constable asked him to go away, hut prisoner struck him in the face. They struggled, and when on the ground the prisoner started kicking him. Witness had to carry him nearly all the way, and when he was in the cell he repeated his attack, again succeed- ins in kicking him. The Bench fined prisoner ss. and costs for drunkenness, and 21 days' hard labour for the assault.—Walter Hill, Walrefield, was also charged with having been drunk, and with assaulting P.C. Wylie,who said that he was on duty, in company with P.S. Brews- ter, on the previous night about 11.59, on Waterloo Bridge. They heard a row on a canal boat at the top locks, and went on the opposite side of the waterway, where they heard cries of " lqu'rder," and calls for the police. Tke constable went to the boat next to the one where prisoner vas, and saw him kick his wife. The moment witness got on the next boat prisoner rushed at him and kicked him. He followed this up with another rush, and this time kicked him with his foot Prisoner tried PO threw the officer into the water, and he again kicke3 him and this time broke his temp. A fine of 10s. and costs was Imposed. NEW YEAR'S DAY:

The year 1896 was ushered in at Runcorn with the customary ringing of bells, the piercing shrieks of whistles at the works on both sides of the river, the hummine of bu27,rs and fn.; horns of vessels, and the noise of swribs, mingled with the strains of music, the carols of singers, and mutual good wishes for a happy and prosperous new year. The watch night services held at many of the churches and chapels were exceptionally well attended, arid when the congregations dispersed shortly after midnight the streets presented an animated appearance. The weather was remarkably mild for the season of the year,and there was a little spitting of rain. At the Parish Church the service was conducted by the Rev. W. H. Stables, and at Trinity Church the Rev. Dr. Preston was in charge of the service. At St. Paul's Wes- leyan Chapel the Rev. T. Bracksnbury was assisted by Mr. B. Crosby, and at Halton-road Wesleyan Chapel, the Rev.' William George conducted. The Rev. T. Brarnall was assisted by Messrs.Beoth aud Hayes at the Primitive Methodist Vree Chapel, Greenwey-road. A watch night serviee was also held at the Seamen's Institute where there was a good number of sailors. Mr. W. Shaw, missionery. conducted the service. Appropriate hymns were sung and addresses delivered at all the places of worship mentioned. Watch night services were also conducted in several of the surrounding villages. Most ot the business places in the town were closed either the whole or part of New Year's Dar,but many of the works went on as usual The chief attraction in the town was the football match between Runcorn and Wakefield Trinity, at' which there was a large attendance.

RUNCOVN COUNTY COURT.

A sitting of this court was held on Tuesday before his honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes. The business of tbe court was very light consisting purely of one adjourned, one default,seven original.and five judgment summonses. An interesting feature of the proceedings was the season- able compliments which passed between the legal pro- fesbion and the Judge. Mr. Burton speaking on behalf of his confreres said that that being the last sitting of the court and also the last day of the year he had much pleasure in wishing him a very happy New Year. He hoped his honour might be spared for many years to preside over the proceedings of that court. He (Mr. Burton) had been practising before his honour for the last fifteen years and he had always experienced pleasure in doing so. Notwithstanding the advanced age of his honour, time seemed to have dealt very leniently with him.—His flonGur said he was very much obliged for the kind remarks and reciprocated them to the legal gentlemen. He hoped that the health he enjoyed might continue. Time probably might have been lenient with him, but he could not forget that he was getting older. But with his present state of health he should continue to preside over the courts, which was a very great pleasure to do.

THE NEW CURATE FOR RUNCORN.

The Rev. W. INTERESTING WEDDING AT OVERTON.

REYNOLDS-BLAIN.

A marriage in which a great deal of interest was taken in Halton and surreunding tow.nships, was cele- brated at Overton Church,Frodsham,on Thursday after- noon. The contracting parties were Mr.Artbur C. Blain, youngest son of Mr. William Blain,of Ashfield,Brom horo', Cheshire,and Miss Kathleen,only daughter ot Mr. Henry Reynolds, J.P., of Norton Lodge,near Halton. The little village of Norton was en fete for the occasion, and there was general rejoicing. The day was observed as a holi- day at Mr. Henry Reynolds' tannery, Astmore, and the employes together with a number of residents had dis- played great iegenuitv in the erection of triumphal arches. One was fixed over the gateway leading to the lodge, and consisted of evergreens and flags and other bunting. On one side was the motto " Long life to the bride and bridegroom," and on the other" A Happy and Prosperous New Year." In the centre were the monograms of the bride and bridegroom. At the entrance to the village from Halton there was another arch on which the following sentiments were expressed "Heal h and happiness to the bride and bridegroom," and " Hearty good wishes for your future life." This arch was beautifully draped with Union Jacks, and de- corated with evergreens. Before the marriage ceremony Mr. and Mrs. Charles Reynolds, of Overton, entertained a number of invited guests. It may also be here men- tioned that on Wednesday evening the workmen at Act- more tannery were entertained to a supper at the Norton Arms Hotel. Halton, served in Mr. Birch's best style. The toast of the " Bride and Bridegroom" was drur.lc with enthusiasm and several others.

Soon alter mid-day on Thursday, ' many were to be seen wending their way to the time-hononred church at Overton, to witness the marriage ceremony. Punctu- ally at 1.30 the bride entered the church, and proceeded down the aisle leaning on the arm of her father, quickly followed lay the bridesmaids. The bride who looked par- ticularly handsome was given away by her father. She wore a dress of rich ivory satin with court train,and the bodice was trimmed with chiffon, orange blossoms, and old lace. She wore a wreath of orange blossoms and myrtle, and an Irish point lace veil, the gift of her aunt (Mrs. Benson). She was adorned with some valuale diamonds, the gift of the bridegrocm. In her hand was a beautiful shower bouquet of white flowers. The brides- maids were Miss Gladys Benson, Miss Rosalind Hutch- ings (cousins of the bride), Miss Gladys and Dora Blain (neices),and Miss Raverslroft(cousin of the bridegroom), Their dresses were of white merveilleux silk with yellow satin yokes, white chip hats trimmed with ribbon and feathers to match,and carried bouquets of daffodils and lilies of the valley. They wore gold arrow and pearl horseshoe bros‘ches, the gift of the bride- groom. The duties of best man were satisfactorily ful- filled by Mr. Fred Blain, brother of the bridegroena. The officiating clergymen were the Rev. H. B. (Blom! (vicar of Frodsham),and the Rev. G. D. Wray (vicar of Halton) The ceremony over, the newly-married couple, and witnesses proceeded to the vestry where the registry was signed. Mr. R. Birkett Musgrove struck up on the organ Mendelssohn's well-known " Wedding March." Hearty congratulations were extended to the happy couple on all hands,and on emerging from the church they were met with showers ofrice.They afterwards drove off to Norton, but on arriving at the lodge gates the horses were unyoked, and a number of workmen and friends dragged the carriage, containing the bride and bride- groom, to the house. A reception was held at the resi- dence of the bride's parents when upwards of 150 guests were entertained. During the afternoon the happy pair left for the Runcorn Station, and proceeded to London en route for the Continent. The wedding equipages were supplied by Messrs. Yarwood and Son, Runcorn. Many valuable presents were received.

THE PROPOSED NEW WORKS AT WESTON POINT.

Itvis now reported that an instalment has been paid by the Castner-Kellner Alkali Company for land at Weston Point upon which they intend to erect works for the manufacture of chemicals. The site consists of over 20 acres, and negotiations have been in progress for some time for the purchase. The plans are in a forward state of preparation,and it may be expected that building operations will be commenced about February. The machinery is to be sunplied by Messrs. Mather and Platt, Limited, Salford Ironworks, Manchester, the principal of that firm heinr a director. The estimated cost of the works is £130,000. and it is intended to erect by successive electrical installations, each complete in itself, up to 4,060 horse power. It is also stated that the company will employ about 300 hands. They intend to manufac- ture caustic alkali and bleaching powder by means of the electrolytic decomposition of salt or alkali salts. The construction of the new works should find employment for many at present out of work, and prove of considerable benefit to the town.

TAKING COLD.

A" Cold "is not necessarily the result of a high or ow temperature. A person may go from a hot bath directly into a cold one, or into snow even, and not take cold. He may remain out in the coldest atmosphere until chilled through, and still not take cold. On the contrary,he may take cold by pouring a couple of table- spoonfuls of water upon some parts of his dress, or by standing in a door, or before a stove, or by sitting near a window or other opening, where one part of the body is colder than another part. Let it be kept in mind that uniformity of temperature over the whole body is the great thing to be looked after. It is the unequal heat upon amerent parts of the body that produces Colds, by disturbing the uniform circulation of the blood, which in turn induces congestion of some part. Keep the feet always dry and warm, avoid draughts of air, keep the system braced up by plenty of sleep and good food, and by a powerful Tonic, and you will be proof against a Cold and its results. The very best Medicine, either for reventing or curing a Cold, is

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

" FREQUENTING " BY A CONVICTED THIEF. At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Friday be- fore Alderman Harrison, Dr. Gornall and Councillor Roberts,Thomas M'Hugh, Bank-street,a convicted thief, was charged with frequenting Scotland-road and other places at 9 o'clock on Thursday night with felonious in- tent.—Detective Sergeant Monaghan in proving that prisoner was a convicted thief said that he was convicted for felony in 1883 when he was sentenced to seven years' penal servitude; he had also been flogged for robbery with violence from a female. On Thursday night witness was in company with P.C. Meacock,and they were watching prisoner and other men ot a similar character from 7.30 to 9 o'clock. Prisoner was in a beerhouse and kept coming out icto the street, and, pretending to be drunk, would jostle people and then ask for money. He cursed one man who refused him, and then went stealthily into a pro- vision shop but the proprietor was there and saw him Witness thought that if prisoner had found a man drunk he and the others would have immediately robbed him. Witness was standing in a dark corner and prisoner came up and said "Can I speak with you" and then recognising him said" Oh it is you is it, Mr. Monaghan." Monaghan replied" Yes, and I want to speak with you," and forth w!th took him to the Police Station where he was locked up. He was watching pri soner from instructions be had received from the Chief Constable.—P.C. Meacock gave corroborative evidence. —The Chief Constable (Mr. L. Talbot) said that at the present time there was a number of these bad characters and shady people in the town who gave the police a lot of trouble, and he asked for the assistance of the Bench in keeping them down.—Prisoner begged to be let off and promised all manner of reformation, but the Chief Con- stable informed the Bench that he adopted similar be- haviour the last time he was brought up.—He was sent to gaol for two month's hard labour. ASSAULT ON THE POLICE.—HEAVY CONVICTION. James Leonardy, 5, Haydock-street, was charged at the Warrington Borough Court, on Monday, with being drunk and disorderly in Berfoot-street, on Saturday night, and with assaulting P.C. Newey,and further with doing wilful damage to the windows and door of the house of George Tingle. Prisoner pleaded guilty to being drunk and doing damage, and not guilty to the assault. P.C. Newey said that he saw prisoner creating a disturb- ance in Kerfoot-street. He was drunk and had his coat air challenging to fight anyone. Prisoner's sister com- plained about his having broken her windows, and 'prisoner then struck her. Witness took him into cus- tody, and prisoner butted him in the chest with his head. George Tingle said prisoner came to his house on Satutday night, and during witness's temporary absence began to assault witness's wife. He (Tingle) put him out of the house when Leonardy commenced to break the windows and also kicked the panel of the door out. Prisoner was committed to gaol for three months. A CONCERTINA PLAYER IN TROUBLE. Albert Forster, 4. Heath-lane, was charged at the Borough Court en Monday. with disorderly conduct in Sankey-street, en December 22nd. P.O Marsh said that at two o'clock in the morning of the above date, the defendant was playing his concertina in Sankey-street in a very loud manner although witness told him he would report him. P.C. Rvlatt gave corroborative evid- ence.—Mr. F. Monks: Where does the disorderly con- duct come in ?—The Chief Constable: I submit that playing the concertina at such an early hour in the morning constitutes disorderly behaviour. I have received many complaints about such conduct.— Mr. Monks: But I question whether that constitutes disorderly behaviour.—Mr. White (assistant magistrate's clerk) : That is for you todecide.—Alderman Harrison: The officers state that the man was warned and yet he persisted in his conduct.—Mr. Birchall : I think that during the past fortnight we have had quite enough of this sort of thing.—Mr. Monks : But suppose that it had been the Salvation Army.—The Chief Constable: But the Salvation Army would not play at that time of the morning.—Mr. Monks : I still question whether the defendant has committed disorderly behaviour.—Alder- man Harrison was of a different opinion,and asked what would be the state of affairs if everyone could play as he liked, as the defendant appeared determined to do.— Mr. Monks: An Act might be passed to suppress it.— The Chief Constable: If one concertina player were allowed to do this sort of thing, 20 more would want to do it likewise. The Chief Constable added that he only pressed for a nominal penalty. He wished it to go forth that the public had no right Ito conduct themselves in such a manner simply because it happened to be Christ- mas time.—Defendant, on being asked what he had to say, said that he carried his concertina under his arm, and he was frequently asked by policemen to give them a tune. (Loud laughter.)—Alderman Harrison : You are evidently a skilled player. There must be a charm about it. (Renewed laughter.)—The Bench imposed a nominal penalty of 15.,1441r. Birchall saying that be agreed with this, simply because the defendant refused to stop playing when requested by the police.

ALLEGED THEFT OF CORLIEFS' TlMM—William Banks. collier, was charged with stealing three collier's picks, valued 3s. 6d.. the property of Benjamin Davies, sometime between the 3rd and 9th December. Banks pleaded not guilty. Mr. It. Stuart who prosecuted. ;said that both parties were em- ployed as colliers by M essrs. it, Evans and Company at Hay- dock, and worked in the same pit, but at different seams. It was the custom of the colliers to bring up their picks to the sharpeners each night, and the next morning, they went for them again. On the 3rd Dtcember, Davies I- ft his picks as used with the sharpeners, but nextrnorning, they were miss- ing, and being unable to find them, he informed the manager who suggested that he should wateh as the picks were taken out in the morning to see who it was that to3k them. Davies accordingly did watch and on the 9th December, he saw the defendant take three picks which he identified as his own. Davies followe t the defendant to the top of the pit, and asked him what he was doing with the picks. He said that they were his picks. Davies then went down the pit in company with the manager, Mr. Wallwork and found another pick belonging to Davies in the place where defendant was working. He (Mr. Stuart) was instructed to press the case, as the company wished to put a stop to this practice which was very common. —Evidence supporting Mr. Stuart's statement was given by Benjamin Davies and Jessie Wallworic.—DefendAnt said that his brother had been using picks. and he did not know what his brother's marks were, and therefore he (defendant) did not know but that they belonged to him. It was simply a mistake.—The bench inflicted a peua.lty of 20s, and costs.

MISERABLE HOME IN HAYDOCK.—George Bannon was charged with neglecting his two children, George Henry, and Mary Ellen, on December 6th and ether dates. Mr. Knowles said th,tthe children were aged respectively three years and five months. Defendant and his wife and family lived at Banks Cottages, Haydock, and he earned 4s 64, a day. In hie house there was scarcely any furniture, and this his wife said was because he spent Ws money in betting. He did not drink, but put all the money he bould get hold of upon race horses. !Us wife would state that she had to get food from her mother, and that her husband had several times taken all the food and elten it himself, in :act he had taken it out of her and the children's mouths, About three months ago he had turned his wife and children out and they had to make their way, saturated with rain to Mrs. Bannon's mother's hause.—lividenec was given by luspector McAteer, N.S.P.C.C., Rachel Bannon (the wife), and P.C. Gresty. The wife in reply to her husband, admitted that when he got 221. 6d. in one week, he gave her £l, and other sums in pro- portion on weeks when he did not work fall time.— After con- siderable discussion, the bench decided to adjourn the case for a month to give the defendant a chance of impreving his home. They said that they wee not satisfied that the wife was blameless as she appeared to get a fair share of the wages.

RUNCORN PETTY SESSIONS.

MONDAT.—Before Mr. R. Imison (chairman) and Mr. S. Taylor. LIEENSIRG.—Peter Millington applied tor and was granted an occasional license to sell at the beerhouse. Ferry Hotel, Church-street, lately occupied by James Hughes Percival. DRUNEENNEBB,—The following were fined for being drunk and disorderly :—William Hart, gardener, Aston, ss. hided. lug costs ; Richard Salkeld. Ws, and costs ; John Salkeld ss. costs ; William Hall, Widnes, ss. and costs. CHARGE OF PERMITTING DRUNILENNES3.—John Snowden, keeper of the Barley Mow beerhouse. Church-street, was summoned for having on the 20th December unlawfully per- mitted drunkenness to take place on his premises. Mr. Berry solicitor. Liverpool appeased on behalf of the defendant. Sergeant Brewster stated that at 9.45 on the 20th of the pre- sent month he was on duty, in Church-street in company withconstable Ridgway opposite the Barley Mow. They saw a woman named Flannigan being carried out of the public i house n a helpless state of drunkenness. She was unable to walk. Witness visited the house and asked the wife of the license why she allowed tha woman to be on the premises in that drunken state. iihe replied that she wasn't so bad when she came in." Mrs. Snowden admitted that she had served the;woman two or three time. with whiskey. Witness asked I her how long the woman had .been on the premises. and she replied about an hour, In company with Inspector Hicks he visited the woman's house at 2. Lock-street. and found her thrown on the sofa unable to move or speak, She was in a helpless state of drunkenness. He again visited the Barley Mow and saw the defendant, and he admitted having seen the woman in the house at a quarter past nine, and to having told his wife she must not serve her with any more. Mr. Snowden then went out, but he had made no effort to get the woman out of his house.—P.C. Ridgway gave corroborative evidence.—lnspec- tor Hicks said at a quarter past ten he accompanied Sergeant Brewster so the Barley Mow, and saw the landlord and his wife. He asked Mrs. Snowden bow she accounted for the affair, and she said "I don't know, the woman went off all at once." He asked her what she had served the woman with, and she admitted the, she had supplied her with two or three drinks of whiskey. She also stated that she did not notice her to be so bad when shecame in. Witness told her that twoor three small whiskeys would not make the woman like that, and she said she could not aceouut for it except it was on account of the heat of the room. Snowden said he saw the condition of the woman and told his wife not to serve her with any more drink, but to gat tier home. He then went out, and when he returned some persons had fetched her away. Witness went across to Flannigan's and found her asleep on the sofa.—For the defence, Mr. Berry said he accepted the evidence for the prosecution almost implicitly as it agreed with his instructions. The witnesses did not see this woman inside the public-house; they had to content themselves with what they considered was her state when outside the house. Both witnesses said she wal being carried out in a helpless state of drunkenness. But the magistrates had to see upon what grounds they formed that opinion—whether they took means to ascertain whether that was so or not. The witnesses assumed that she v as in a state of drunken- ness and took no further means to ascertain that she was drunk beyond going to the houses where they found her asleep on the sofa. The evidence was not conclusive. Mr. Berry quoted a case, Somerset v. Wade, in which it was held that a licensee could not be convicted of permitting drunkenness on his premises unless he had knowledge that the person in question was on his premises, and was that knowledge permitted him to be served. He hoped to be able to satisfy them that the moment the licensee knew the woman was there the best means were

taken to get her home. The woman who was alleged to be drunk lived a few doors away from the public-house, and when she entered it on the night of the 20th about eight o clock in company with a butcher narned Watson, she was perfectly sober. Mr. Watson and the woman had a drink together. and after Watson left, she was supplied with one other think. There was no one else in the room. About twenty minutes afterwards Mrs. Snowdon entered the room and tound her in a faint ing condition. She had fallen down on the side of the street and was lying with her head supportd by her hands. Mrs. Snowden sent for her husband and he ' came in company with his son. She was still in a fainting condition when they removed her, and the fainting was pro- bably caused by the heat of the room. It was two or three hours before she recovered, and her husband was of the opinion that She was not drunk when she was taken home. The wife of the lic ,nsee stated the circumstances very fairly to the police. If she had told them that the woman had sot been served the police would have had no case at all. There had been no evidence given to show that the licensee knew the woman was drunk,—Bvidence for the defence. corroborat- ing Mr. Berry's statement was given by Mrs. Snowden, Mrs. Finnegan, Mr. Watson, and Mr. Bernard Finnegan. The lat- ter witness thought it was weakness that his wife was suffer- ing from at the time.—The magistrates retired to consider their decision, and after a short interval the Chairman an- nounce,: that thy thought Wore was an element of doubt in the case and would dismiss it. They would ask the Superin- tendent to consider what action he wou'd take with regard to the summons against Finnegan.—Superintendent Okell said if the megistrat-s asked him to withdraw it he would do so.— The Chair.na I did not think the suggestion should come from the Bench.—Superintendent Okell said he would not call any more witnesses. In his opinion the woman was drunk.—The Clerk advised the Superintendent to withdraw the summons against Finnegan, and he dli so.

VACCINATION.

THE REGISTRAR-GENERAL'S REPORT. FOR 1895.

SBOULD BE EXAMINED AT A. PUBLIC MEETING

" Over 1,000,000 children were vaccinated during the year. That was the report fr,r 1894, and the number is likely to be exceeded for 1895. Without charming that vaccination does or does Hot prevent smAll-pox, it would be well, considering that this vast number dehildren, who are to take our places in the future, have been vac- cinated without their consent, that a public meeting shuuld be called to consider whether something may not be done at once to lessen the danger to the health and and lives of these little ones, so lone; as vaccination is compulsory. We all know that vaccination is the cause of many diseases, which are transmitted from arm to arm, and as we are compelled to be vaccinated because the majority of the nation believe that it doss protect us from epidemics of small-pox, may we not adopt such measures as will reduce to a minimum the amount of disease so transmitted, or, what:would be better, find an absolute preventive of these diseases, and perhaps a cure for those already contracted. This is a, subject of vital importance to us all, and, as a Physician and the head of a tamily, as well as a patriotic British subject, I would like to see a public meeting held in every town and city in the land to consider the Registrar's report for 1895, as soon as the report appears. Dr. Wilson, after making the above statement, ap- pealed to me for my opinion. I assured him I fully endorsed all he said, and that if such meeting could be held I would attend and make certain suggestions, which I felt sure would be approved by the meeting. "Then you have already been considering this sub- ject ?'' said the Doctor. "Yes," I answered," and with mest satisfactory re- sults, for while my first two children were seriously and dangerously ill through vaccination,the last two (twins, as you know) came through the ordeal without giving us the slightest worry gr cause for anxiety. I would, therefore, be pleased to state at a public meeting th means by wliich this was accomplished." "Do you object." he said, " to explaining them to me at once ?"

"Certainly not," I answered," and, fortunately, the best explanation I can give you happens to be in this parcel which I hold in my hand, for I am just returning from the Chemist's, where I have been to purchase this bottle,which I am giving to my gardener for his infant, who is to be vaccinated next week."

While I spoke, I opened the parcel, and showed him a bottle of Vaccination Virus Vanquisher. or V.V.V.

"What !. a patent medicine?" asked the Doctor. "No, nothing of the kind," I answered. " but the prescription of an eminent doctor, who has been in practice for forty years,ani who is.l find,after thorough investigation, a physician most familiar with vaccina- tion, and a man of many years' experience. He used this prescription with such excellent results that be finally decided to place it in the hands of a Cornpa.ny.by whom it could be put upon the market in exactly the same form as he had prescribed it." Whether the public meeting will be held I cannot say, but I have convinced the D3ctor that it is to his interest to give V.V.V. a trial, and I well know that the results will be mo3t satisfaetory. No remedy was ever more urgently needed, and I certainly hope to see the public meeting called. To be had of all Chemists at 24. 9i. per bottle.or from the Va.cciaation Virus Vanquisher Company,Limited,3l, Snow Hill, EC., who will send a pamphlet gratis to all who apply. •

BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL.

ANNUAL TEA. MEETING.

On Wednesday the annual tea party and entertain- ment in connection with the Bold-street Wesleyan Church Sunday Schools was held in the schoolroom. About 100 scholars and friends partook of the repast which was provided at 5 30. At 7.30 the entertainment commenced under the chairmanship of the Rev. 0. Watkins. There was a fair attendance and the room had been made attractive by flags, Japanese lanterns. and evergreens. Mr. J. Lingard. the secretary, read his report in which he stated that the work of the past year had gone on satisfactorily. The number of scholars on the books was 440,and the average attendance was 217. Sixty-five scholars merited prizes for full attendance, and 302 scholars and 38 teachers were members of the church. The amount collected for missions by the scholars was £3B 10s.. all in half-pence. There were 418 books in the library and 28 readers. Mr. Siltock, the superintendent, was going to remain another year and they were also pleased to state that many tried teachers and officers were still amongst them and they hoped that those who had been careless in the past would bestir themselves and put forth every effort to further the interests of the scholars. They were thankful to the ministers for taking an interest in the scholars and especially to Mr. Mason who had assisted them by his presence and wise counsel. The Wesley Guild was a succe.ssful part of their organisation, as also was a number of other ventures.

The CHAIRMAN briefly referred to the season, and wished everyone a happy ROW year. The Rev. G. D. MASON said that means were being taken to re-organise all the Sunday school libraries in the circuit, and the work of re-organisation was in exceedingly good hands—those of Mr. Parker, of Pen- keth. _ _ _

A varied programme was then gone through, induct. ing a pianoforte duet by Mrs. Mason and Mrs. Pigot ; two action songs by the juvenile scholars of the school entitled" The Japanese Fan," and "Lullaby" ; violin solo hy Miss Winnie Pigot ; songs " Two Cities," and "Light in the Darkness," by Miss Jennie Jones ; songs by Mr. W. H. Wallington ; two humorous readings by Mr. H. M. Ashton entitled "Montpellier's Recitation," and " A Visit to the Tower " (Artemus Ward). Miss rigot accompanied. All the items were well rendered, and did credit to the performers. The usual votes of thanks and the singing of the Doxology concluded the proceedings.

TURKISH TOLERANCE.

Not a littln has been said about the professional tolera- tion of the Turks towards other creeds, but to show the real feeling entertained it is only necessary to cite the Moslem statutes, in which Christians are designated as " dogs " and "pigs." And in the burial certificates Christians are not said to be dead but "damned." For example, a burial certificate attested by the British Am- bassador certifies to the priest of an Armenian church that "the impure,putricl,stinking carcase of N-, this day damned (deceased), may be concealed under ground."

He shaves the prisoners in Moyamensing Pri.on, and one stipula- tion in his contract is that he shall net speak to the pri- soners or hold communication with them in any way.

FOOT is

NORTHERN RUGBY UNION. WARRINGTON v. OLDEIAM.

The return match between these two teams was played at Watersheddings, Oldham, on Seturd ay. It will be remembered that at the fir.-.t meetine of the clubs,which took place only a few weeks ago. Oldteine were victors by two tries to nil. Oldham hive Si/00. n brilliant form since that date, and it was therefore not with very great feelings of confidence that the Warrington team travelled to °Winne on Saturday, e:pecially as the two close attentione of the Leigh playere on Thursday had incapicatated no fewer than six pla.yere, viz., Heesom, Burton, Cross, Taylor, Hill, and Swift. This being the case, the m itch almost seemed a gift to Oldham. Heesom's position was very difficult to fi.l, and it was only at the last moment that it was decided to play Scott as full back. The teerne were as follows : Warrington : Scott, fall back ; Barber, Lawless, Carey and Broady, threeqeartere ; Clark and Houghton, half backs ; Fairhurst, Wilcock. Scho'ze, Burrows, Nevins, Thornton, Barrett, and Areirewe. forwards. Oldham : Eagland, full beck ; Bradhurr, Taylor, S. Lees. and MeCutcheon, thieevartere ; Varley and A. Lees, half hacks ; Bonsor. France, Hughes, Merrill, J. Lees,'Etchulls, Whiteley. and Phillips, forwards. Mr.Slevin.of Wiean,olfieiated as referee. The weather was of the most miser ible descriptioe, and as a conse- quence the gate was the smallest of the season,there be- ing only about 2,0.1.70 present. The rain came down ia torrents during the whole of the matcleand accordingly, exciting incidents were conspicuous by their ahsence. Varley won the toss, and he set Warrington to face a blinding storm of rain. Wilcock kicked off, and the re- turn by Eagland being very poor, Warrington were quickly on the Oldham line, and several scrimmages took place in dangerous proximity to the Oldham goal. Eagland at length relieved by a long kick to the centre of the field, when Scott mulling the bill, Joseph Lees and Etehulls were on to him betere lie could recover himself.and the ball was dribbled to within a few yards of the line, when Barber saved just in time by kicking in touch. A line out took place and the bell was taken over the line. A scramble ensued for possession by both teams and the Warrington players made the mis- take of trying to kick dead, instead of dropping on the ball. Several attempts were 'nide to kick dead,but the feet of the kickers unaccountably failed to touch the bell, and in the midst of this. Joseph Lees dropped upon it, and thus a very fluky try was secured about three minutes from the commencement of the game. The place was an easy one, hut Eagland kicked in the direction of the dressing room instead of the goal posts. After the restart a smart dribble was made down the field by Burrows, Scholtze. and Houghton, and this trio was not stopped until they were within an ace of scoring, Eagland robbing them at the critical moment. A prolonged attack was now made upon the Oldham lines despite the wind, and a good run by Barber almost ended in a score. The attack was at length brought to a conclusion by a long kick in touch by Varley. and Etchulls and Merrill were very prominent after the line out by a dribole to the quarter flag. Nevins and Burrows neutralized this and returned the ball to centre. Warrington were playing the best game under the circumstances by dribbling,as the ball was too sodden for passing, Burrows, Thornton and Broady supplemented the advantage gained by Nevins and Burrows, and the quarter flag was reached. From the ensuing scrimmage, Houghton followed the kick up so well that he tackled Eagland on the line, and for a time a score seemed certain every moment. Oldham at length got back to the quarter flag, when Burrows by an indiv- idual dribble, the finest of the afternoon, threaded his way through the Oldham team and got the ball over the line. He was just in the act of grounding it when Sam I Lees rushed up and kicked dead. Hard lines indeed for Burrows. Varley restarted. and Carey returned it well, and from now to half time,Warrington attacked strongly but could not penetrate the defence, and Oldham led at half time by a try to nil. Varley restarted the game, and aided by the wind, Warrington attacked with great persistence. A flying kick by Eagland in front of the posts might have been utilised to advantage had Lawless made his mark, but that player preferred to try to get round his opponents. Finding this impossible, he tried a drop at goal and it only just went wide. From the drop out, the Oldham forwards dribbled to centre, and S. Lees getting posses- sion made a long run before he was collared. As the run was across tho field, this was of no advantage to his side. Oldham however attacked strongly after this, and the Warrington defence had an anxious time. A free kick fell to Oldham, and Eagland taking the place, the ball fell a little short, and Broady cleared the danger. Houghton caused the attack to be brought to a termina- tion by a smart kick in touch, and Clark, Broady, and Burrows placed their side on the offensive by a good dribble. The Oldham forwards again came away wth a rush, but Scott proved the stumbling block to them this time. Soon afterwards he stopped another rush. Warrington again got the upper hand, and Barber had a grand chance of dropping a goal, but he passed to Carey instead, and the latter was tackled before he could be dangerous. The remainder of the game was occupied by spasmodic visits to either end, Warrington baying the lion's share of the attack, and on one occasion Eagland was tackled by Houghton on the line. No scoring occurred however, and the drenched players were heartily glad when the whistle blew for full time ,Oldham thus leading as at the interval, by a try to nil. The opinion of the spectators, both Oldham and Warrington, was that the game ought to have been a draw, in fact Warrington had the best of the game and the try scored ought never to have been scored. The Warrington forwards overplayed the Old- haulers, and as a consequence, Clark and Houghton showed up quite as well as Varley and Lees. The day was totally against threequarter play. and Lawless was about the best of the Warrington quartette, though there was very little to choose. Full back does not suit Scott, but it must be remembered that the position was &completely new one to him, and that he has never played in first class company before. He saved several rushes, but he is much more at home as centre three- quarter.

MANNINGIIAII V. WIDNES.

Played at Manninghana before 1,000 spectators. Barber commenced operations for Widnes, and their forwards at once rushed into the Manningham quar- ters, but a timely kick by Lorimer transferred play to the other end, where Proctor scored a clever try which Lorimer converted. The home team renewed the attack, and Lorimer scored, but the same player failed at goal. Splendid kicking by Manningham kept Widnes on the defence, Pickles fielding the ball smartly dropped a beautiful goal. The visitors played up in a spirited manner, which caused the home team to concede a minor point. Manningham, however, once more showed their supeziority, Proctor and Needham running in tries, both of which Lorimer improved, thus giving the home team 22 points lead at half-time. Barra- dough restarted the second half, and a lot of uninterest- ing play at mid-field followed. At length Manningham again took up the attack, and Barraclough was all but over. The visitors, however, relieved, and the ball was kicked to Brown. He ran strongly and transferred to Atkinson, who made further ground and then gave the ball back to Brown, who scored a brilliant try. Lorimer's shot was a little wide. The game after this was of a scrambling character, but the visitors played up a little better and attacked for a time. Their efforts were, however, of no avail, and Atkinson got possession of the ball and ran down the field. Lorimer took his pass well, and ran over between the posts. Proctor took the place and landed an easy goal. Towards the close of the game Nevitt ran down the field and passed to H. Peters, who scored for Widnes. Barber failed at goal. Score : Manningham 5 goals (1. dropped), 3 tries (33 points), Widnes 1 try ;3 points).

Myers took the place, but failed to add the additional points. A free kick to the home side enabled them to gain further ground, and reach the Runcorn " 25 " for the first time. Leeds were thus two tries behind when the game had only been in progress about ten minutes. Gayter touched it down, and was actually in possession when the referee came up, it ended in Surnmersgill claiming and being awarded a try. No goal resulted. Myers had now come on again, and took his position at centre, but it was seen that he limped considerably. Leeds now played up hard their f lwards keeping the slippery ball going along the ground. Buttersvorth's defence again proved excellent, and the game was driven into the home half by the visiting forwards. Warder kicking too far when iniend- ing to find touch, sent the ball into touch-in-goal, a pro- mising attack by Runcorn thus coming to nought. Wal- ton losing the ball the visitors got it into their hands, and brought off another good passing bout, as the result of which Leeds had to defend. Darkness was now sett- ing in fast, and it was with difficulty that individual play could be distinguished across the ground. Runcorn were having the best of matters, but were met by good defence on the part of the Leeds team generally. At length, as the:result of a long kick by H. Hainstock,from which the ball rolled into touch, Leeds got a footing in the visitors' quarters. They could not, however, main- tain it, and the superior play of the Runcorn backs changed the scene once more, and the slippery ball being kicked over the line Walker fumbled it, with the result that Gayter shot up and scored his first try. Myers could not land the goal, but Runcorn seemed now safe from defeat, and 70 odd spectators who had journeyed with them broke out into a song on the position. A fur- ther point was to follow,for some exceedingly smart pas- sing by J. Faulkner, Robinson, Myers and Warder en- abled the latter to score a splendid try—the best of the bunch of four so far recorded. The goal bick was another failure. Leeds had another spurt, and Summersgill dropped at goal to no purpose, and the home men re- tired beaten by a superior team, the following being the result :—Runcorn, 4 tries (12 points) ; Leeds, 1 try (3 points).

RUNCORN v. WAKEFIELD TRINITY.

On New Year's Day Runcorn had as their opponents the Wakefield Trinity team, and although there was hardly an element of doubt as to the result, it must te admitted that the Runcorn team, considering the fifteen they placed on the field have commenced the year well. The Trinitarian's position in the combination table is anything but an enviabla one, and when one recollects that but a few years ago they were the Yorkshire champions, one is inclined to remark "How are the mighty fallen." The team they brought from Yorkshire on Wednesday may be considered of a representative character, although they have placed stronger teams in the field this season. Ducker was a substitute at full back, and the remainder of the team was as follows : Three-quarters. Howell, Griffiths, Lockwood, Dolan ; half backs, Morgan, Brakewell; f orwards, Burnley, Crossland, Price, Eyre, Allchurch, Meorhouse, Walton and Jackson. Runcorn placed a very indifferent team in the field, drawn largely from the "A " team ranks ; the fact being borne in mind that a stiff fight is in store for Saturday when Oldham are to visit Runcorn, and when probably the most important match of the season in connection with the Northern Union will be contested. It was after 2.4 s—the time fixed for the kick-off when the visitors arrived but they had taken the wise course of donning themselves in their football outfit in the railway saloontand thus saved most valuable time. There was a large attendance when the teams faced each other and the visitors set the ball in motion. The first scrimmage of the day took place in the home quarters, but Gayter receiving a pass brought relief to the homesters by a good kick. A free kick was shortly afterwards acceded to Runcorn,and good use being made of it, further ground was gained. The visitors were pedned near their own lines for some time, and at this point of the game Brakewell was hurt, and retired for a short time. Jackson and Moorhouse brought welcome relief to the Tykes, and forced the game into Runcorn territory. J. Faulkner, however, placed his side on the aggressive again by a capital run, and passing to War- der that player almost got over. Ducker by means of a free kick relieved the pressure a little. Butterworth made a smart return, but Brakewell neutralised his effort. The next noticeable feature was a passing movement by the visitors, initiated by Morgan, and taken part in by Griffiths, Lockwood, and Dolan. The latter player was knocked in touch when almost on the line. The visitors now played up with remark- able vigour but they failed to break through the home defence. West, who throughout the game played promis- ingly, made a good run along the touch-line and trans- ferred play to the visitors domain. Warder was next prominent with a smart piece of play and passed beauti- fully to Gayter who had hard luck in not scoring. Run- corn continued to press for some time, but the Trinitar- ians defence was invulnerable. Ducker again brought relief to his side, and the whistle shortly afterwoods sounded for half-time, and the teams changed ends with a clean sheet. W. Faulkner restarted.and a good return was made by the visitors, Butterworth punted strongly, and a mull being made by Ducker and Dolan, almost let Runcorn in. The home team now severely tested the visitors' defence,but by short stages the Tykes gradually worked the ball out of danger. Runcorn again rushed to the line, but the effort ended in a futile dead ball. From the kick out, the homesters by several short rushes in which Warder was conspicuous, again made tracks for the line. The visitors, however, continued to play strongly and Brakewell made a useful run. Play now became of a more open character and J. Faulkner at length crossed the line amidst much jubilation. Warder failed at thel kick. Success again attended the efforts of the Runcorn team almost directly afterwards for Woodward gaining pos.session,he passed to W.Faulk- ner, and that player handed the ball to Gayter who scored a meritorious try. Farmer did not succeed in converting it into a goal. With this second reverse, the visiting team seemed to fall away in their play entirely, and the homesters were not slow to take advantage of their opportunity. They again made tracks for the visitors' lines, but a pass forward caused a scrimmage opposite the posts. The ball was heeled out to J. Faulk- ner who quickly romped round the scrimmage and scored the third try of the match. Farmer took the kick, but the ball struck the posts. It was not very long afterwards before disaster again befel the visitors. West was prominent with a good run after receiving a pass from Warder, and W. Faulkner was successful in bringing the effort to the desired result, scoring near the posts. Farmer again took the kick,and augmented the score by two points, by kicking a goal. The visitors played with a little more dash after this, and a passing movement between Lockwood, Griffiths, and Howell gained a great deal of ground. Butter- worth, however, was equal to the occasion and saved finely. Shortly afterwards the visitors were acceded a free kick, but Moorhouse's attempt at goal was any- thing but a success. Runcorn again entered the visi- tors' domain, and West had hard lines in not scoring. The whistle soon afterwards sounded with the score standing I—Runcorn 1 goal, three tries (14 points) ; Wakefield Trinity, nil.

WARRINGTON " A " V. OLDHAM "A."—The Oldham "A " team visited Warrington on Saturday to play the return match. There was a fair gate. A heavy rain during the game made it difficult to get any speed on the ground. Soon after the commencement Lawton passed to Rhodes, who sent the ball to Fillingham. The latter scored a try, but it was not converted. Warring- ton took the ball to their opponents quarter line. where Hughes obtained possession and made a dash for the line. Just as he got there the ball was kicked forward by his opponents. On resuming, Savage got away nicely and cleared his opponents when he was called back, to the great disgust of the spectators. The visitors got posses- sion of the hall and made a move, but they had not gone far before they had to return. Warrington again made a rush for the line, and this time Foden succeeded in scoring. The kick at goal miqsed, the ball bouncing _outwards off the post. This greatly encouraged the home team and they played hard. Anotker try was almost scored but the visitors combined and rushed up the field, where Moores saved, and returned the ball to half-way. Oldham again took the ball towards the home goal but Price sent it back and Plinston took it to the Oldham quarter, where he was overtaken by Fillingham. he whole game he and Hughes played well, and often broke away, but the nature of the ground was against good running. Warrington pressed strongly during the latter part of the game, but could not alter the score which was, Warrington, one try, Oldham, 1 try. Teams: Warrington : Spruce, full back ; Plinston, Price. WARRINGTON ST. MARY'S V. BROUGHTON RANGERS " A."—This match took place at Broughton, on Satur- day afternoon, in a downpour of rain. Both sides were well represented. RUNCORN FREE LIBRARY LECTURES.

CHARLES KINGSLEY.

A lecture under the auspices of the :Runcorn Free Lib- rary was given in the Technical Institute on Friday evening. There was a good attendance. Mr. J. Wilding, surveyor to the Runcorn Urban Council was the lecturet and his subject was "Charles Kingsley." In introducing the lecturer, Mr. E. Marshall wished all present the compliments of the season. He sincerely trusted that the mining year would be a very bright and prosperous one, whether taking it in the capacity of citizens or individuals. Referring to the lecture he said it was a tempting subject. He was one of those who had an enthusiastic admiration for Charles Kingsley. In his opinion, as a social reformer and writer he stood in the very first rank. It could be freely said of him as of his master whose birth they had been celebrating, that he had compassion on the multitude. His great heart was full of sympathy for the sufferings of the poor, and he laboured unceasingly on their behalf. He thought he laid to heart the motto, that it was better to wear out, than rust out. That night they bad to deal more parti- cularly with Kingsley as a writer than as social reformer. A great many people held that too many books were written,but he thought it might be said of Kingsley that it would be a great loss if he had not written. His spirit still lived and worked amongst men. Referring to the building in which they were met, he said the preliminary object of technical instruction which was being given in that institute was that the youth of the town might make the best of the world from a material point of view. But that was rather a narrow view, and he (Mr. Marshall) who had to run that institute took a broader view of technical education than that, and concurred in the view of Lord Bacon who said that knowledge was not an establishment for barter or sale but a rich store- house for the glory of God and the relief of man's estate. (Applause.) Referring to the lecturer, he said before any- one could deal with such a subject as the one before them he must have a scientific knowledge and a.passicn for literary pursuit. He was sure they would find that Mr. Wilding possessed that combination. Mr. Wilding at the outset of his lecture commented upon the fact that no century had been so rich as the one in which we lived. Referring to Kingsley be said he had stood before them as one who came from a very old school. He was moreover brought up in contact with everything that would expand his imagination. His early aquaintance with Clovelly brought him into con- tact with that strong English life, depicted in " West- ward Ho " and other works. After briefly mentioning Kingsley's successful college career he prmeeded to refer to his becoming an ordained minister at Eversley. Pro- ceeding to deal with Kingsley's life work be typified him as one ef those great men who bad given us the civiliza tion which we now so much enjoyed. We enjoyed, he said, the benefits of an advanced sanitary conditions, and the name of Kingsley would long be associated with the names of those who waged war with ignorance. He was very largely the cause of bringing about the Town's Improvement Clauses Act and similar sanitary reforms. As an example of the ignorance prevailing in Kingsley's time respecting sanitary matters, the lecturer referred to the characters introduced into " Two years ago." Proceeding he said the charm of Kingsley was that of a conqueror. Ho was never satisfied until he had reached the .object he had in view. Commenting upon Kingsley s identification with the chartists he said Kingsley could see the rights of their case. He felt very strongly and endeavoured to lay down the statement of the case so that both sides should understand it. In identifying himself with the chartist movement he braved losing some of his friendship and braved the opposition of the press. He wanted what was right and fought for it. There was quite a charm in considering the way he dealt with the Chartis ts themselves. He helped to open the minds of those who were dealing with the question and we owed much to him for the privileges we enjoyed to•day. Referring to Kingsley as a writer, he said from his early youth he had been constantly writing, and it was impossible for one to take up eny of his novels with- out believing that Kingsley wrote what he felt. An a writer of history many people had found fault with him, stating that he took the skeleton and filled in to his own form. The lecturer spoke of the control Kingsley man- aged to obtain over his students, which showed that he created a fullness around the history of early times that commended itself to the student's mind. He was a most prolific writer as an essayist, and his essays were remarkable for their intensity of vigour, freedom of thought, and beauty of execution. He was also a scient- ist and the lecturer commended his stories of town ' geology, which were sufficiently homely to interest the people who usually abjured the science of geology. Referring to his clerical duties he said Kingsley was , appointed Canon of Chester, and whilst in that city he delivered scientific lectures on geology. Thence he was transferred to the canonry of Westminster, which posi- tion he only held for two years. His death occurred at Eversley in 1875—a centre where Kingsley passed.away from the home life of the Eversley people, to live in the life of every Englishman who had the privilege and opportunity of reading his works.—On the motion of the Chairman, seconded by Mr. Jones, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to the Lecturer. and a similar com- pliment was paid to the chairman, on the proposition of Mr. Imison, seconded by Mr. Beck.

The lifeboatmen who lost their lives in the great gale on Christmas-eve while trying to resue the crew of a ship that had stranded in Dublin Bay were buried at Kingstown on Saturday. The procession of hearses was accompanied to the cemetery by representatives of every rank and creed. The men who were drowned at he wreck of the Moresby were buried at Dungarvan.
