BATTLES OF THE FUTURE.

Modern warfare is no longer confined to our brave, red-coated battalions. The fertile brainef the inventor '-rnust first design the weapons with which the soldier flghts and on the good qualities of which his life so greatly depends. The inventor and the soldier are alike responsible for the slaughter of modern engagements,and there is mutual rivalry between the two. The one is Stimulated by the hope of " glory " and the other seeks for pecuniary and possible social distinction. New con- ditions are continually demanding new developments and in no department of science has more progress been effected, or more money spent, then that which deals 'With:the construction of ordnance and war-like material. Since, when, the "villainous saltpetre was dragg'd out of the harmless earth,' old methods of warfare have been entirely superseded; the archer has laid aside his trusty bow and the stout yeoman his pike. The arque- bus of the old neusketecr has lens; since disappeared, and joined fellowship with those old favourite companions of chivalry—the made' and maul. The cast iron guns used by Wellington at Waterloo have been long since replaced by steel guns of larger calibre and of tremen- dous destructive Dower. In small arms, projectiles shells, &c., extraordinary innovations have been intro- duced, and all nations are busily engaged in a jealous rivalry for the possession of the most powerful military equipment. And the inventor must keep pace with the increasing demands on his activity regardless of cost. Battleships and 100-ton guns cost fabulous sums to build, yet, acting on the wisdom embodied in the maxim "si Ins pacem, pare bellum," this gigantic outlay is to be .excused. Certainly none of the great Powers can afford to be taken at a disadvantage by its neighbour's superior armament. Aa instancing the great expenditure with modern con- ditions entail we may mention that, whereas the cost of the old cast iron guns was £2l per ton, the modern steel (Krupp) gun costs £l7O per ton. There are shells now Fade that weigh 7001b5., and mortars capable of project- ing a weight of 2,9861b5. a distance of a mile and a half. Such a result could never have been achieved by using the old-fashioned noisy and smoky gunpowder and gun- Co ten, nitro-glycerine, and cordite are some of the new sutes. A great development has also been effected With the introduction of " smokeless " and noiseless Powder which was used effectively in our recent Chitral eampeign. But the most interesting feature in the "battle of the guns" has not been touched upon in our brief review. To follow the progression of the rifle through its various stages of development would require an article to itself. The arquebus. the flint-lock, and the "Brown Bea" eesed against Napoleon were useless as weapons of preci- enon, and it was not till the introduction of breech-load- lug guns that this defect was remedied. A competition of rifle inventors, instituted by the Government in 1866, led to the combined inventions of Messieurs Henry and Martini, being adopted as the national weapon for many Years. Owing, however, to the increasing demand for a 'quicker firing rifle this old favourite is likely to be dis- eerded altogether in favour of the Lee-Mitford Magazine rifle with which our soldiers are being equipped. These modern small arms are wonderfully different !rem the weapons we have been considering. • The new rifle shoots as fast as a man can pull the trigger. It is effective at a distance of a mile, and the lead bullet, clad In a steel coat, leaves the muzzle at a speed of nearly half a mile a second. The idea of the up-to-date rifle is to throw a small and very hard projectile with enormously increased velocity to a much greater distance with greater precision than formerly. Just here comes in an odd point. The weapon is an improvement in the direction of humanity. The bullets travel so fast that apparently the body does not attempt to resist it, and it passes through the flesh or bone with- out inflicting much of a shock, making a small clean hole and not breaking up the tissues. It goes through the thinner bones, as the shoulder blades, the ribs,and breast bone without smashing effect. An opportunity for a first-rate test was offorded by the recent Chitral campaign in India. Wounded natives With tiny holes drilled clean through them, were out and about within twenty-four hours, not suffering any ser- ious inconvenience. Such :was actually the case with a Majority of the Swazis who were hurt in various engage- ments. This might be understood more easily if only the , flesh had been injured, but bones were perforated and yet the men were not disabled. Sharpshooters, armed with the modern style ef rifle Will play a great part in future warfare. The discharge of the new weapon is almost noiseless, and, smokeless Powder being used, there will be nothing to betray the location of a concealed rifleman who pours a continuous fire with murderous accuracy into the ranks of the enemy. The commander in the field will see his officers and men fall around him without knowing whence the blow is struck. Two or three skilled marksmen will be able to pick off every man at a battery of artillery, so as to render the guns of no use. The new rifle fires ten shots from the magazine as fast as the trigger can be pulled, and quick as a wink another set of ten cartridges is substituted for the empty shells. The cartridges are fastened together in strips. The invention of smokeless powder has given cause for the creation of a contrivance with a purpose quite Opposite. This latter device is called a smoke bomb. In battle, bombs of this kind are to be thrown from field mortars toward the enemy. Bursting, they set free Clouds of thick black smoke, which, being jest about as heavy as the atmosphere, haugs like a veil for a long time and forms a dense screen, impenetrable to vision. Behind such a screen en entire army could reanceuvre unseen. A division, for example, might be split into two parts and assail in flank and rear, as under the cover of night, a hostile force that was expecting to be attacked in front. * In the battles of the past, and after the introduction of cannon and gunpowder, so dense a cloud of smoke commonly was spread over the field that the combat- ants on bath sides were only able to fire at random. In the warfare of the future it will be very different. Kill- ing will be done with scientific precision and certainty. Formerly the sharpshooter was regarded as a, rather cold-blooded type of soldier, inasmuch as his object was to take the individual life deliberately and with set pur- pose; but he was a gentle and harmless leant com- pared to the destroying marksman with the smokeless and noiseless gun.—" Science Siftings."

MATRIMONIAL AGENCIES. BY THE AUTHOR OF "AN ENGLISHMAN IN PARIS."

The clever writer of "Anecdotal Europe" gossips very pleasantly, apropos of recent police-court proceed- ing in London, on Parisian Matrimonial Agencies. He Proved them to be very different from the English Variety. He writes :—All that the Paris agencies of the beat standing undertake to do is to bring the parties together, not only oy means of epistolary correspond- ence, but through personal introduction ,• and this is so may say icately—ma skilful!naged that neither del the parents nor N•elitives of the girl, nor she herself ,have the slightest suspicion of it being a planned thing. My statement holds good even in the case where the girl's friends have had recourse to the agency. Each agency publishes a bemonthly journal, which is sent to all the railway bookstalls, hotels, clubs, and cir- culating libraries throughout France, especially and often to smaller establishments across the frontier. Its price, post paid and in a sealed enyolope, is one franc per copy. The subscription to the agency is fifty francs, which the subscriber may take out in advertisements, if he likes, and in consideration of which he is entitled to correspond with any or all of the advertisers in the paper until the supposedly right partner for life is found. In the event of a marriage being contracted through the agency's intermediary, the agency claims two and a half percent.from the bridegroom on the marriage portion of the bride, or a sum fixed beforehand in the reverse ease. The recipient of the letter knows the writer's name ; the writer is absolutely ignorant of the recipient's. The whole of the correspondence passes through the hands of the agency, which supplies special envelopes for the purpose. But there was no necessity whatever to advertise. The candidate for wedlock may apply personally to the direc- tress of the agency—it is generally a woman. That is what I did when I wanted materials and facts for my. articles on the subject. I paid my fifty francs, stated my qualifications and prospects. Next morning I had a letter asking me to call at the office of the agency. " I think I have got what you want," said the principal. And then slee gave me full particulars of the young girl whom she bad in view for me. Neither the girl nor her parents had the remotest idea that the agency was bestirring itself for her happiness. I was to see my possiblefiancee two evenings later on at an " at home!' The Principal herself was to take me. From two or three remarks she dropped. I felt convinced that she knew pretty well all about my position, except the important fact that I was a married man. I did not absolutely carry my marriage certificate on my back.

Two evenings afterwards the principal, who might have passed muster in the best society, fetched me in her wn,or what seemed her own.carriage ; and in about o half-an-hour I found inyself in the apartment of a gentleman holding a by no means subordinate Govern- mental appointment. I knew him by sight; he did not know me. lie was simply subsidised by the principal of the agency. Subsequent experiments taught me that he was one of a hundred. I was introduced to the young lady and her parents. Of course, next morning I pre- tended to have changed My mind. " I would remain a bachelor," I said, " for another twelve months or so." But I took the trouble of verifying tho principal's state- ments. Every one, including that of the ten thousand c pounds dowry, was strictlyorrect. Wheth( r I should have been accepted by Beatrice or her parents if f pur- sued the affair to the last stage but one is another thing. I doubt it, for everyone of my statements would have been also sifted by the principal, and there were a good Many that would not have

CURIOSITIES OF DESCENT.

_Abraham Hayward, the famous "Quarterly" Re- viewer. once thought that he would like to have some ancestors, so he walked straight to a picture-dealer's. Selecting a portrait of a cavalier in half armour, with features not quite unlike his own, Mr. Hayward made a bid for it, but, deeming the price asked too high,he went on his way. A few days later Mr. Hayward went to dine with Lord Houghton, and was astonished to find the picture in the dining-room. Seeing that it attracted his guest's attention. Lord Houghton said : " Very good picture that ! Came into my bands in a curious way. Portrait of a Milnes of the Commonwealth period—au ancestor of mine." " Ah, indeed 1" said Mr. Hayward, "he was very near being an ancestor of mine."

DEATHS IN THE W/SLEYAN MINISTRY

DURING 1895.

The deaths in the Wesleyan ministry during the year now ended have been 28, and included in this list are one ortwo junior men. The most prominent name is that of the Rev. Dr. Greeves, who was President of the Conference when it met in the Potteries in 1884, and for some 40 years was a distinguished preacher in the Methodist Church. Tens of thousands in and out of British Methodism will also know that the death-roll of the year contains the name of Peter Mackenzie, who was everywhere beloved. The Rev. Frederick Payr e, who spent no fewer than 41 of the 57 years of his ministry is the Liverpool district.did a remarkable work in the way of chapel building in North Wales ; also in the promotion ot middle class education. The Rev. Thomas B. Good- win was at one time financial secretAry of the Leeds dis- trict ; he was an able preacher. The Rev. George Ken. yon was a native of Wath-on-Dearne, and spent 14 years in Yorkshire circuits. To Wesleyan Methodists the full list will have an affecting interest. The figures in paren- t lesis denote the deceased minister's age :—George Ken- (ss), William Elton (92), Frederick Ewer (68), John G. Devis (44), Henry S. Yates (41), Henry M. Radcliffe (53), Joseph Chapman (80), S. Wesley Lawton (59), Frederick Greeves. D.D. (62), Samuel Atkinson (79), William H. Lewis (78). Joseph Simpson (70), James Monahan (50), Herbert W. Haime (73), George Abbott (71), Joshua Johnson (55), Isaac Jones (71), John Scott (65), Frederick Payne (80). Elias T. Dickenson (39), W. G. Pascoe (57). Thomas B. Goodwin (80), Richard Wil- liams, Rhyl (67). Thomas Withington. Bristol (80), Ben- jamin Smith (59), Peter Mackenzie (71), W. Woodward Spencer (51), Samuel Blakeley (58), Singular to say,the number of deaths and the average is as near as possible the same as last year. For some years past the average age of ministers who have died In the Wesleyan ministry has exceeded 60 years, which fact speaks well, consider- ing the increasing wear and tear of the itinerant system.

A PEER'S RESIDENCE SOLD FOR

It is not often that the residence of one of" our old Pobility " realises so small a sum as £l9. Yet this was a sum which the residence of the late Lord Donington fetched by auction a few days ago at Farleigh Hunger- ford, a few miles from Trowbridge. The residence in question is not the historic Farleigh Castle, but it is the chalet which the late Lord Donington caused to be erected about twelve months ago in a field on the road from Farleigh Hungerford to Norton. It appears that after his lordship let Farleigh Castle to a lady from Liver- pool he came to the conclusion that U. e air of Farleigh Hungerford suited him—notwithstanding he possessed mansions at Donington Park, Leicester Bowden Park, Leeds ; and Loudoun Castle in Scotland, &.—and he gave instructions for the erection of a chalet in a field close to the park. The erection is entirely of wood, with corrugated iron roof, its aimensions being 27ft. by 25ft., and is divided into four rooms, one answering for a kitchen, in which is a cooking range. Here his lordship lived for some time, but he died last July, and the " tin hut" (as the villages termed the place) was soil the ether day to a gentleman acting on behalf of the Trowbridge Baptist Church, for the sum above named, for the pur- poses of a village chapel in the neighbourhood.

AMERICAN POLITICAL CARTOONS.

(PRODI TUB " ST. JAMES'S GAZETTE.")

The English people ha ye a poor opinion ef the news- papers of the United Sta tea; but whoever will spend a few hours over the files brought by the last mail will be favourably impressed with the dignity, reserve and talent displayed by many American journalists when a national crisis demands sobriety of thought and re- straint of language. There are exceptions, of course; some newspapers indulge in wild and threatening words, and clamour for war with feather-headed levity ; button the whole, Great Britain has not much to complain of at the pens of the American press. There seems to have been a genuine desire to consider the question of the Guiana boundary in all its bearings, and—given the patriotic point of view—to weigh well the consequences of precipitate support of President Cleveland. The journal that takes the lead in the fulness, thoroughness, and impartiality of its news, and the sanity of its com- ments, is the New York "World," which is the property of Mr. Pulitzer. This newspaper, in the same issue as that which contained the Message, denounced the latter as " preposterous Jingo Bugaboo," and, as telegraphic advices have shown, has since striven with migitit and main to undo the mischief caused by it and to make con- flict between the two peoples impossible. The service this paper has done to the American peo- ple by the courage of its comments and the effectiveness with which it has marshalled all the facts it could ob- tain bearing upon the controversy, has been heightened by front-page cartoons which "hit off" the situation , with humour as well as exactness. The " Bugaboo " article was pointed out by an illustration showing President Cleveland as too big for his boots. There 'stands the President—a massive, square-built gentleman in a buttoined frock coat—looking out upon the world with defiafft pride and pointing to a placard which he lholds in his left hand : " President Cleveland's Mes- sage : Venezuela must be protected at all hazards." But the whole pose and figure is made ludicrous,for with up- turned trousers the President is standing in "J. G. B." , boots, whose uppers his huge fait have split from toe to I toe. " Blaine's Shoes Filled at Last" is the satirical footnote to the picture, and in the corner is a drawing of a pair of old boots "Left by J. Blaine," one named " Jingoism "and the other " Bluster," one boot occu- pied by Senator Lodge and the other by Senator Chandler, each politician frantically waving weapons and in a condition of violent verbal explosion. The car- toon of the following day represents President Cleve- land,with a tremendous strain on the chest-buttons of his frock coat, striding into the bush. He has just flung down the gauntlet—a glove bearing the words, "Monroe doctrine,"—and in his left hand he carries a rifle, on whose stock are the significant dates 1776,1812. Behind him is a rabble of pigmies, each bearing a caricature of a Jingo politician ; some mounted on asses, others draw- ing loads of tin soldiers, and all in a frenzy of acclama- tion and jesticulation. " rhrowing down the Gauntlet: A New W arrior to the Front," is the title of this cartoon.

Perhaps the most effective and humorous cartoon is one that appeared when the war-fever was at its height —on Saturday the 21st—before the full effects of the panic in Wall-street the day before were realised through- out the States. It is entitled " After the war is over— Is is worth a war ?" Jonathan, with long hair and a long goatee, iu a ragged dress-coat, a star-spangled waist coat, and torn pants, has copped down on the ground in a sitting pasture. His right hand is on his forehead, after the manner of a man regaining consciousness and realising that he is in a frightful fit of some kind. The face is expressive of terrible anxiety, to which a clue is given by strewn documents bearing the words" War Debts." To Jonathan's right sits John Bull—huge of body, shore of leg, au.l of aldormanic rotundity. John Bull is in riding-boots and leathers, with the national flag for waistcoat, and a coat whose skirt is torn to shreds. In appearance he is an apple-faced personage— clean shaven double-chinned, swollen as to the cheeks, bridgeless as 'to the nose, and bald save for curling hair over his ears. Be has strips of sticking-plaster pasted ouer his forehead, is holding the elbow of his right arm as though hurt on the funny bone, and his right eye is "bunged up." About his feet" War Debt " papers are strewn. On the whole—due probably to his immensity of girth and the length of Jonathan' a reach—he seems to have got slightly the worst of the tussle •. though Jonathan, with sticking-plaster across his left cheek and his nose slightly out of the per pendicular, looks pretty bad. At the feet of the two is a miniature figure of Venezuela—a grinning military gentleman in a large three-cornered hat. He is dancing merrily, while the giants who have been fighting about him are bswailing their injuries and their debts. Yet another cartoon is labelled "Playing with Fire." It represent a court-yard flanked with houses containing dynamite, powder, fire- works, and other combustibles. In the yard a group of boys—Cleveland, Lodge, Chandler, Reed and others—are throwing fresh fuel on a blazing fire. Of cartoons on the other side, mention should be made of a typical one in the New York " Tribune," which, however, in an editorial, advises its readers to " keep cool." It gives a picture of a landing-stage. President Cleveland, in what passes in America for a shooting- a c k has just landed from a boat, and is kneeling be- tore csident Monroe, who stands over him grasping ept , r the United States flag with his left and bestowing a sort of episcopal consecration upon the head of Cleveland with his right. By the side of Cleveland are a gun and two dead ducks—an allusion to the belief that Cleveland had ten days' duck-sbooting before he determined upon issuing his Message. This cartoon is headed " The Re- turn of the Prodigal." Of the letterpress in the newspapers there is so great and varied an abundance that Wits difficult to arrive at a fair estimate of the whole. The extracts from the leading journals telegraphed to London day by day have already given some idea of the trend of American opin- ion. So far as one can judge:, after reading many columns of opinions collected from journals, great and small, in all parts of the States, these conclusions impress them- selves on the mind :—" That American journalists, a class, are film believers:in the validity and necessity the Monroe doctrine. That they are divided in opinion as to the reasonableness of the extension of its original meaning given to it by President Cleveland. That they are adveise to war with Great Britian. That even those who most loudly champion Venezuela's claim doubt whether Venezuela gives sufficient cause for quarrel— whether Venezuela proves a sufficiently good pretext for the assertion of the Monroe doctrine as formulated by President Cleveland. That they are fully conscious of the defenceless condition af their coasts, and of the terrible injury they would suffer as a nation even if they were victorious, which they consider highly problemati- cal. That they do not think Lord Salisbury will yield as to the territory up to the Schomburgk line. That if the Commission does not report in favour of Great Britian up to that line they do not see how war is to be avoided in the end. That, nevertheless, their sheet anchor is in diplomacy, not in armaments." These are but rough conclusions, drawn from the reading of many columns. It is not necessary that chapter and verse should be cited for them ; but it, should be said that thry relate to the period before the subsi- dence of the war fever, and in their essence, to judge from extracts since telegraphed, do not seem to have undergone modification.

The largest mass of pure rock salt in the world lies under the province of Galicia, Hungary. It is known to be five hundred miles long,twenty miles broad, and two hundred and fifty feet in thickness.

KING EDWARD THE THITtli AND ARMENIAN ATROCITIES.

A letter of protection granted:by King Eiward 111. to three Armenian Christians in the year 1360 affords (says the " Times") a striking proof of the antiquity of the Armenian question and the unchanging character of the problem which still awaits solution in 1895. When the King was at Reading, in the month of April in the year of grace 1360, to him and his Keener came the piteous lament of three worthy Armeuians, brothers Stephen, James, and Anthony, of the Order of St. Basil. Their true tale commended them to the charity of all good Christians. The kingdom of Armenia, at least for the greater part, had been wrested from the hands of the Christians by Pagans and other worshippers of idols and the land was occupied by the intruders. Not content with victory, but desiring in their wickedness to extin- guish the Christian faith, they waszed the churches, and never ceased to most cruelly slaughter the Chris- tians. Wherefore these pious monks could no longer dwell in peace in their native land,and had come to pray the King for leave to dwell for a while in England, and to seek the alms of the faithful wh‘s might be moved to pity on hearing of the horrors wrought in far-off Ar- menia by the cruel Mussulman. The King grants the prayer of the three Armenian monks. He takes them " under the wings of his Royal protection," being inclined thereto by the piteousness of their case and by the reverence due to God and holy religion. Neverthe- less (though evidently this was added by the scribe as part of the common form of such letters), the King's protection is to continue only as long as the bearers of the letter do nothing to the hurt of his Grate or of his faithful subjects, and bear themselves in true faith and honesty.

Ertterfs to tbt Orittor.

COUNCILLOR BENNETT ON THE UNEMPLOYED AND IRELAND.

Sir.—lnconsistency! thy name is Bennett. Mr. Bennett says I am never happy (sic) unless I am figuring in the correspondence columns of some local paper. Well, in the first place as to my never being happv,Mr. Bennett does not know me, or he would never say that! And then, in the second place, I fail to see that it is any business of Mr. Benuett's—this figuring of mine in the local papers—unless, he does me the very great honour of allowing me to " figure " in the columns of his illus- trious journal. But Mr. Bennett, while he pretends to denounce my mode of becoming famous, says he is sorry that he has not neither the time or the inclination to emulate me in this pleasing pursuit ! But he will ! He then informs your readers that the electors of Howley Ward are quite capable of exercising the franchise with- out any assistance from me. Well, the electors of Howley Ward did not seed Mr. Bennett into the Couricil Chamber to represent them! If he happens to be the representive of Howley Ward, he is such on account of a breach of political etiquette, or in com- mon parlauce, be won by a fluke. But I premise him faithfully,thatfiflGod spares me until the next November election time comes round, the electors of Howley ward will—without the permission of Mr. Bennett—have some assistance from me, and I flatter myself that we will give Mr. Bennett more time to " become famous," in another sphere of action.

Again Mr. Bennett very kindly points out to =2 e, "that i is usual to fight one's battles out upon the spot and to say what one bas got to say where it can be ' promptly answered." Now, Mr. Bennett will pardon me, but I must point out to your readers. that Mr. Bennett is misleadiag them, for if Mr. Bennett will try and extricate himself from the fog and maze of his new Conservatism, he will doubtless have some faint recol- lection of the fact that I did answer him ! and that some a the members of the debating society, cried shame on Mr. Bennett's devoted head on account of his indecent remarks on the poor and the afflicted ! I would remind Mr.Bennett,that I an an Irishman ,and that:it was never yet known, that an Irishman deserted a" spot "where there was a chance of a fight of any kind whatsoever. Mr. Bennett further charges me with" misrepresenta- tion." Will Mr. Bennett deny that he applied the word "nuisance," to the unemployed, and to the inmates of the workhouses ? True, it was not so reported in the local press, hence, the necessity of my writing upon the subject. No one doubts, that Mr. Bennett is a most estimable gentleman, that he has a sympathy for the poor, and is admirably fitted to occupy any -(private) station in life. But one may be reasonably excused for concluding, that a man who is so imprudent as to dub his fellows a" nuisance," is not fit to hold any public position whatever ! and that the sooner he is sent to adorn the domestic circle, for which he is so admirably fitted, the better for himself and all cpncerned ! Mr. Bennett is quite mistaken in concluding that I have no remedy but the workhouse for the unemployed problem. As I told him at the meetiag of the Debating Society, I advocate the remedy stated by " Nunquam " in" Merrie England." I cannot help pointing out the inconsistency of protesting against discussing questions, which have been discussed in the Debating Society, in the press. If it is wrong for me to discuss them in the press, it must be wrong for Mr. Bennett to do the same ! In conclusion, let me add I have no desire to spend my time in "tearing my hair ''over the cruelties perpe- trated by Englishmen cn my beloved country. I leave that soul-inspiring exercise to the more congenial tem- perament of our dreaming friend, the famous author of "The dream of an Englishman." But I assure him that I will make the best use of whatever poor ability the great God has endowed me with in repairing so far as I possibly can the sad, the appalling results of such fear- ful persecution! And to this end I would call upon my fellow-countrymen to organise themselves into a solid and compact body, so that they can make their power felt at the proper time. I say to the unemployed, to all the workingmen of Warrington, but especially to those located in Howley Ward, remember this word for Mr. Bennett—" Nuisance "—and do your duty like men.— Yours, &e., J. C. DALTON.

===== 0002601_18960104_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
A TALE OF A TUE.

Sir,—A recent writer in the " Nineteenth Century " engaged in the discussion on the alleged Agnosticism of the undergraduates, spoke of the freshmen coming to college as " prejudiced in favour" of the popular re- ligion. And this is so. Children are steeped to the lips in religious prejudices, and all reasoning on such sub- jects is forbidden as a danger to " the faith." And the natural result ensues. Hence we see acute business men— men who have succeeded in business solely by means of the logical employment of" reason " in that business— suddenly suffer a complete change, and become the vic- tims of unreasoning belief, when the subject of religion comes on the carpet. Such men. so to speak, seem to keep a vacant plot at the back of their mental business premises—a sort of lumber-yard in which to pile the most incongruous and incompatible religious notions. Here, among the rejectamenta of saner minds, he can revel in vague indefinates, and indulge, unchecked by his business hour reason, in the dear delight of thinking that 2 and 2 may sometimes make five, if the exigencies of reconciliation demand it. An excellent illustration of the ill effects of this prejudicial training is afforded by " Amos White." in last issue. This gentleman—if it be a male indeed, the hysterical style almost forbids the thought—has been so thoroughly steeped in these preju- dices.as to be incapable of re asoning himself free from them.. He 'brings his back-yard to the front with a vengeance. I have not the advantage of knowing our " Amos" and therefore, for aught I know, his front may be all back. As a substitute for reasoning he has off by heart two sets of names. One set— Voltaire, Hume, and Paine—he keeps for indiscriminate vilification, the other set for equally indiscriminate adulation. If our friend would, for one brief hour,leave off his hysterical and exclamatory style, and come down to the consideration of facts, he might learn something to his advantage. Instance: If the scales of prejudice could fall from his eyes he might learn that no such per- son as "Jesus Christ " ever lived upon this earth! Jesus of Nazareth was a Jew, and most certainly would not have answered to the Greek appellation. Christ—Kris- tos—the Anointed was a posthumous title which Jesus never, in all probability, ever heard as applied to himself. It is the outcome of Greek mysticism and genius for fab- line. Now, Amos the candid, is like most other Chris- tians. He has evolved from his "inner consciousness" a theory of Christianity which is as like the real article as law is to equity. Calvinism is the most logical and Biblical form of sectarian Christianity. The murderer of Servetas reduced Christianity to a focus, and accen- tuated its horrible dogmas. Modern refinement rejects Calvinism as too atrocious for entertainment. And, when such as Amos White "rush in where wise men fear to tread," ani assume the role ot defenders of the faith, it is their own dearly beloved misconception of it, the creature of their own brain whose cause they so valiantly defend, and not the dour and immoral reality that con- demns " unbaptized babes a span long to eternal torment." Finally, as to his " matter for thought" where do "the precepts of the new Testament teach men to abolish slavery ?" Nowhere in the Bible, from title page to Colophon, is the abolition of slavery advo- cated, or spoken of as an evil to be got rid of, instead of which we have the tramp's maxim,"take no thoughtifor the morrow."—Yours, JOHN PEERS.

OLD FOLKS TREATS AT WARRINGTON.

COCKHEDGE MISSION-OLD FOLKS TREAT.

Perhaps one of the most successful events ever held in this place was that which took place on New Year's flay, the occasion being an Old Folks Treat promoted by the select class of males, under the patronage of Mr. Z. Armitage and Mr. Arthur H. Lee. The " treat" took the lines of a sandwich tea and first class entertainment. From 3.30 the good old people of the neighbourhood began to assemble in the chapel where selections of music were discoursed by Messrs. Jackson (W) and Beardsall (piano), and Adcroft (clarionette) prior to ad- journing to the schoolroom for tea. The place had been suitably decorated with bunting, banners, and mottoes bearing the inscription "A hearty welcome to the old folks," &c. The tables, which were heavily laden with the viands provided, were relieved by window and hot- house plants, kindly lent for the occasion by Mrs.Adcroft and Mr. Griffiths, and when the aged people (upwards of 200) rose to sing "Be present at our table, Lord," the whole presented a cheerful scene long to be remembered. After tea the old folks returned to the Mission Hall, where there commenced at 6 30, under the presidency of Mr. John Plinsten (superintendent), supported on the platform by Messrs. Brown, Jackson (George), Robin- son, Sudlow, Clare, Gallop, and Cox, a carefully pre- pared programme. Songs were rendered by Misses Jackson and Rushton, and Mr. S. Hassan ; recitations by Messrs. Phcenix and Lowe; concertina solos by Mr. William Smith; duets (vocal) by Messrs. Butterfield and Jackson (J), and a dialogue " Paddy's Mistake," which created much merriment, by Misses Cousins and Utley and Messrs. Butterfield, Lowe, Phcenix and and Jackson (J). Mr. Walter Bellian also sung four humorous songs in his own refined, accomplished and inimitable style which were richly enjoyed by the audience. Every item on the programme was performed in an excellent man- ner and fully deserved the manifestations of approval with which it was received. Mr. William) Jackson ably officiated the entire evening on the pianoforte and har- monium, and about 9.15 the old folks dispersed well satisfied and very profuse in their expressions of grati- tude for the "grand treat" which had been afforded them.

A coloured lion-tamer named William Beaumont died yesterday morning in St. Bartholomew's Hospital, Lon- don, from injuries inflicted by a lion during a perform- ance in the Agricultural Hail on Christmas Eve.

RUNCORN URBEIN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting of this Council was held no Thursday morning at the Town Hall, Runcorn. Mr. E. Imison, J.P., occupied the chair, and there were also present : Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, Messrs. F. Wiswall, P. Speakman, J.P., C.C., J. Jones, T. Dean, G. H. Kirk- ham, J. Pritchard, Wilson, J. W. Lightburn, G. Hewitt, H. J. Grice, W, Collier, M. Sweetirian, D. Biabrown, R. H. Posnett, Mr. F. A. Lake (law clerk), Mr. Marshall (secretary), and Dr. McDougall (medical officer). "A HAPPY NEW YEAR."

The Chairman said before proceeding with the busi- ness he weuld like to wish every member of the Council and the officials " A Happy New Year." He thought they were met under rather more favourable conditions than in 1895. The cloud of trade depression showed some signs of lifting, and he hoped that work might be more plentiful this year for those willing to do it, and that Runcorn might have a return of the prosperity experi- enced in years past. To the members of the Council, the officials, and the public generally he wished a happy and prosperous new year.—Mr. Bisbrown said he was sure they all reciprocated the wishes of the chairman, and hoped that the good old town of Runcorn would be more prosperous this year than in the past.

CANAL BOATS.

A report was submitted by the Secretary regardiag the execution of the ainai Boats' Acts. During the year nine boats had been registered, five of whin were new boats.and four were registered owing to structural alter- ations, which made a total of 935 boats registered at Runcorn. The Inspector had riF•ired 300 boats. There were found on board the boats 486 men, it° women ,and 119 children. There were no cases of sicknesi notified under the Infectious Diseases Notification Act, 1889, as having occurred on canal boats. A satisfactory feature in the returns were that only 110 women were found on board the boats as against 130 for last year and 119 children as against 151.

MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT.

Dr. McDougall reported that the number of deaths registered during the past mouth amounted to 19. Two were inquest cases, and of the remaining 17,6 were due to bronchitis,2 to kidney disease,2 to mesenteric disease; 2 to premature birth,and one each to abscess, scarlatina convulsions, syncope, and one to debility. The 19 deaths showed an average death rate for the twelve months of 12 per thousand of the estimated population and exclu- sive of inquest cases of 107 9 deaths were amongst children of twelve months or under. 43 births were rPgia. tered. For the corresponding month of last year, 21 deaths were registered an 7 deaths on whom inquests were held, total 28.—Mr. Pritchard said the report seemed to point to a low death rate.—Dr. McDougall : I think the doctors may all take a holiday. (Hear, hear.) Dr. Gastaldi asked whether scarlet fever was not preva- lent in the town.—Dr. McDougall said it was of a very mild type, and deaths were very few. There was a negligence cn the part of same of the residents to advise officials of cases. Although it was a mild type, the cou- tagion was equally the same.

THE SURVEYORSHIP OF RUNCORN.

The Highways and Buildings Committee reported that the following resignations have been received from the surveyor and assistant surveyor.—" The Chairman and Members of the Runcorn Urban District Council. Sirs,— Having regard to the re-arrangement of my duties and salary as recommended by the Highways and Buildings Committee I beg respectfully to say that I fall in with the said arrangement and accordingly tender my resign- ation as your surveyor, sincerely thanking you for all kindness rendered during the 42 years of my service. It has been my desire to be faithful to my trust, and this principle being inherent I think will continue. I have the honour to be your obedient servant (signed) MICHAEL BARER."—"TO the Chairman and Members of the Run- corn Urban District Council. Sirs,—ln view of the re-ar- rangement of the surveyors' department in which your committee recommend my appointment as surveyor I hereby beg respectfully to tender my resignation as assis- tant surveyor. Yours obediently (signed) JAMES WILD- ING." The Committee recommended that the aforesaid reaignations be accepted,and further recommended that Mr. Wilding be appointed surveyor and water engineer at a salary at the rate of £lB2 for the first two years, and that Mr. Barker be appointed consulting surveyor and superintendent of the outdoor work connected with the duties of surveyor, at a salary at the rate of £144 ner annum, and that such appointments date from the 2nd January, 1896; that the Council hereby convey to Mr. Barker their high appreciation of his long 3nd faith- ful service to the town as surveyor and that the seal of the Council be affixed to an address expressing such ap- preciation. Mr. Bisbrown in moving the adoption of the recom- mendations, said he thought it was superfluous to make any remarks in regard to the recommendations. It was a well-known fact that Mr. Barker felt unequal to the duties of surveyor, and wished to be free from some of the responsibility. The committee had very carefully gone into the matter, and their deliberations lasted for an hour and a half, so that it could not be said that they had come to a hasty Conclusion. They had in Mr. Wilding an officer who was well qualified for thl position of surveyor in every respect, and he did not think they could have a better one. The rearrangement wculd be a saving of a yearly sum of £4O. Mr. Barker's duties would now be chiefly outside werk, and free from a great deal of the strain be had had hitherto. The pro- perty owners would have to pay the greater portion of his salary, which would come out of private street funds.

Mr. Speakman seconded the adoption and the recom- mendations of the committee with great pleasure. He believed Mr. Barker had done his work nobly and well for a long period, and they wished that his health might be spared to continue work of roads' surveyor. The re- organisation was for two years and there would be a saving of £4O a year. It would be very satisfactory to the ratepayers to find that their interests were being looked after, and that their retirinc, surveyor would be earning a salary. He would not dwell further on the matter as the whole thing was gone through very care- fully in committee...._

Rev. Dr. Gastaldi:proposed an amendment, stating that it was the desire of the committee who went into the question to save something to the ratepapers. He did not think they had succeeded in saving the amount which the ratepayers expected. He therefore proposed that the surveyor should receive £156 a year instead of £lB2. and that Mr. Barker be paid £ll4 per annum in- stead of £144 or-nosed. By doing so they would save the ratepayers £lOO 4 year.--,Mr. Sweetman seconded the amendment, and said he did not think the sum of £39 as proposed by the committee was much of a say- ing.—Mr. Lightburn supported the original proposition, and in doing so said Mr. Barker was relinquishing over £7O a year in order that the present responsibility might be removed from him. So far as the actual duties of the surveyor were concerned, no man could take up at once all the threads of the surveyors duties. There were thousan is of pounds worth of sewers, drains, and pipes buried beneath the foundations, and they would be able to refer to Mr. Barker who would 'be able to give any information desired. He thought it would be a great loss to the town if they had not Mr. Barker to refer to, as under his superintendence, the town, or the greater portion of it had been built. 011'lr. Wiswall spoke of the increased duties which devolved upon the surveyor by recent acts of Parliament as, for instance,the model building bylaws and improved sanitary measures which had to be enforced.

Mr. Pritchard thought the committee had -struck upon a happy medium in the fixing of salaries. He pointed out that Mr. Wilding held certificates from the Science and Art Department, and was engaged teaching at schools in Runcorn and Liverpool at nights, for which he derived about £lOO per year, and he thought it would be unreasonable for them to expect him to give his ser- vices for the sum of £156 per annum. Referring to Mr. Barker, he said he had given the hest part of his life in the service of the town, having been their surveyor for 42 rears.

Mr. Wilcnn also expreaced regret, that Dr. Gastaldi had thought it desirable to divide the Council on the matter. The work could not be carried out by one man. If they discharged Mr. Barker they would have to give about the same amount to a complete stranger.—Mr. Grice thought that Mr. Wilding was a very efficient man for the position. With regard to Mr. Barker he thought they were only doing an act of justice.—Mr. 'Collier spoke in support of the resolution. He said they were dealing with a gentletnan and one of high principles. Mr. Bar- ker need not take one penny less than the salary which was previously fixed. In coming forward as he had done, it showed that he was willing to adapt himself to the necessities of the times.— On being put t. ehe vote only two, Dr. Gastahli and Mr. Sweetman voted for the amendment, the rest. voting for the proposition, with the exception of Mr. Kirkham who dianot vote.

Mr. Kirkham then rose and moved a further amend- ment that the sum of £l2O be paid instead of £144, and £lBO in place of £lB2. Ile said if Mr. Barker had been such a splendid and faithful servant he had been paid for his services. Fit! had only done his duty. If he had earned more, it Was not a matter for them to consider now; it was a matter which should have been considered by the old Board. He did not consider Mr. Barker was now worth more than £l2O a year. and what was paid over that sum was a pension. They paid wages equal to any private firm, and he did not think there was a firm that would give a man over 70 years of age a sum of £l4O a year.—Mr. Speakman : Question.—Mr. Kirk- ham : Thank you, but I think it will stand questioning. He thought a stonemason or builder would do the work for a less sum.—Dr. Gastaldi said he would second the amendment, inasmuch as he lost his own. (Laughter.) In taking the course he did, he did not cast any reflee- tions on the capabilities of the two men, hut he conzid- ered that the pay Was toe much.—Mr. Lightburn , ridiculed the hypothesis on which the amendment had been based, speaking particularly of the idea to get a bricklayer or stonemason to do Mr. Barker's work. He referred to the increased cost in the surveying depart- ments of other authorities as compared with Runcorn. Mr. Barker's salary was being paid 1-y property owners in the making of streets.—Mr. Wilson said there was an idea abroad that Mr. Barker uas being pensioned off without working at all. He wished it to he understood that he was going to do outside work.—Mr. Speakman said Mr. Barker would do everything he had done in the past of the outside work but would n)t be held respen- sible in the technical way as when he was surveyor. He thought that when a member of the Council brought a surveyor to the common level of an ordinary artisan-it was begging. the question.—The amendment was lost, only Mr. 'Kirkham, Dr. Gastaldi, and Mr. Sweetman voting for it.

FITA.LIT I. ON TIII SIIP CANAL.

THE RESULT OF A COLLISION.

Mr. J. E. Worsley, coroner,held an inquest at Runcorn Police Station on Monday afternoon, or. tho body of Henry Smithson, captain of the steam tug Wigan, and residing at 25. Smith Bank Terrace, Runcorn, who met with his death on Friday night,on the Manchester Ship Canal, near Weston. Mr. Newman. solicitor, Manches- ter, appeared on behalf of the Manchester Ship Canal Company. Robert Smithson, son of the deceased, engaged on the tug Wigan, was the first witness called. He stated that the deceased was 47 years of age,and was captain of the tug Wigan. About 5.30 on Friday night he was on the tug when the accident happened. They were going round the bend of the Ship Canal near Weston on their way up the canal,when the Wigan ran into the Florida,a steam tug going in the opposite direction. He saw the Florida's lights before they struck. His father blew the whistle twice for the Florida to go on the other side of the canal but it was too late. Both tugs belonged to the Ship Canal Company. He did not think his father altered the course of his tug after he had blown the whistle. The Wigan struck the Florida on her port side, and another collision afterwards took place between the Florida and the Cambria with the result that the Florida rebounded and struck the Wigan. The shock threw his father overboard, and he did not see him come up again. It was about five minutes afterwards when his father was recovered. Cross-examined by Mr. Newman: Don't you know that two whistles mean that you are going to starboard helm P—The Coroner to witness : This is an important question. you have said it meant that the Florida should make way for you. Witness said he thought that was so, and that the Florida should take her port side. In answer to another question by Mr. Newman,witness said that the Canabria was about three or four yards behind the Florida. Did you see the Cambria before the whistle was blown P—l cauldn't see her lights.—When the two whistles were blown you hadn't seen the Cambria then P—lt was on account of the Cambria that the whistles were blown.—But you have just said that you hadn't seen it. Harry Saunders, Abergele-street, Runcorn, driver on board the Wigan, said he was below at the time of the accident. The first signal he received was" half-speed," the next "stop," and the two following ones "astern," and " full speed astern." He did not notice any signal given. The first collision occurred when he got the order to stop, and the second collision when going full speed astern. The engine stopped and he went to the screw. He found the captain in it. It was that which caused the engine to stop. Dr. McKenzie stated that one of the crew of the Florida came for him a little before six on Friday evening. The deceased was brought up the canal to Runcorn where he met him. His heart was beating when he first examined him and he endeavoured to restore him. He attributed death to drowning and not to injuries. Thomas Fearnett, 4, Regent-street. captain of basge number 9, stated that he was standing aft on the barge at:the time of the accident. His barge was tied alon.- side the Wigan. He saw the Florida approaching, and she was showing her right light at the time. She was on the starboard side, and the Wigan was also a little to the starboard. He heard the Captain of the Wigan give two sharp blasts with his whistle which was meant that he was going to starboard helm and he followed that up. Therlorida kept its richt course, it was not the duty of those on board to alter their course after the signal given. A collision occurred and the Wigan struck the Florida on the starboard. The Cambria which was following on the Florida came up and struck her, with the result that the Florida collided with the Wigan. He heard the lad shout that his father was in the canal. He could not say whether it was the wheel or the shock which knocked him overboard. If he hadn't starboarded his helm there would have been plenty of room for the Florida to pass. The Coroner: You consider it was an error in judgment ?—lt was a gross mistake. He had known the deceased for three years and was quite friendly with him. James Harrison, Northwich, captain of the Florida, the property of the Ship Canal Company, said he was at the wheel at the time of the collision. They were pro- ceeding down, and the Wigan up the canal. As they neared the Wigan her captain gave two short blasts with the whistle, and the Wigan proceeded starboard helm, coming right across the canal. A collision then happened. If deceased had not starboarded his helm the Florida would have passed clear. He could not say what was the deceased's idea in doing so. He supposed the deceased thought he had time ti get to the starbiard side before they passed him. It was an error of judg- ment. If he had kept hie own side of course there was plenty of room for the Florida to pass. He had known the deceased for five years. In answer to a question by Mr. Newman, witness said it would have been useless even if the Wigan had crossed the nose of their vessel. The Coroner said it was one of these puzzling cases which seemed to frequently occur in nautical affairs. It seemed as though there were times, as in the case of the captain of the Wigan, when nautical men seemed tolose their nerve and presence of mind, and do that which every other nautical man as soon as he sees it condemns, and which condemned itself immediately, afterwards by causing an accident. He could only conclude from the evidence that the present was indeed one of those occa- sions. The real cause of death was collision, and the collision was certainly caused by the deceased causing the vessel Wigan to go into collision with the approach- Florida. Tne deceased seemed to have been a man of experience,and knew his duties thoroughly well. He had committed an error of judgment, and through that the accident happened. He did not think there was any blame in any shape or way with the captain of the Florida. and certainly no blame attached to the Cam- bria. The jury returned a verdict of " Accidentally drowned."

NEW YEAR'S HOLIDAYS AT EARLESTOWN.

For the New Year holidays the Viaduct Works and Vulcan Foundry closed on Tuesday night until next Monday morning. Messrs. T. and T. Vicars closed on Wednesday only, and all the tradespeople in the town closed their premises on that day also.

EART.ESTOWN WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL.

The junior and senior scholars had a social even- ing on Thursday night in the Wesleyan schoolroom. The juniors were regaled with fruit,&c., and passed the evening in games, &c. The seniors at 9.30 sat down to an excellent supper, and afterwards en- joyed themselves in games, &c. EARLESTOWN SELECT DANCING CLASS.

The first annual ball in connection with this class took place in the Town Hall, Earlestown, on Tues- day evening. There were about 50 couples present, and dancing which commenced at 9.30, was kept up with spirit until about five o'clock on Wednes- day morning. The music was supplied by the Earlestown Orchestral Society, and the M.C.'s were Messrs. Matthews and Tonks. BITTEN BY A DOG.

On Monday afternoon two large collie dogs were fighting in Cross-lane, Earlestown, when Inspector Morgan tried to separate them. A young fellow named Hibbert, employed by Dr. Mouncey, as coachman,went to the assistance of Inspector Mor- gan, and after choking one of the dogs off,tbe other returned to the attack, and bit Hibbert on the band very severely. He at once proceeded to Dr. Mouncey's and had his injuries dressed, and it is hoped no serious results will follow.

NEW TEAR HANDICAP AT THE CONSERVATIVE CLUB.

Tho. handicap at billiards and skittles promoted at the Close of the year at the Conservative Club, Earlestown.were terminated on Tuesday night. For billiards there were 51 entries and there were 13 prizes. Mr. F. Brierley secured the first, Mr. J. Gray second. Mr. B. Fairhurst third, and Mr. J. ITowe fourth. There were 71 entries at skittles and 10 priz-s. J. Green first, B. Fairhurst second. W. Mawdsley third, C. Dickenson fourth, and .T. Smith (Crow-la,te) fifth. The prizes consisted of geese, ducks, rabbits,and liquor.s,and were distributed the same night by Mr. J. Catterall, chairman of the committee. EARLESTOWN GOOD TEMPLAR&

VOLUNTEER NEW YEAR'S SHOOTING.

The annual shooting competition promoted at the new year by the " G " Company (Newton) K.L.R. volunteers took place at Highfield Moss range on Wednesday. There were 63 contestants, all of whom obtained a prize of some description. The conditions, were 5 shots at 200 yards, and the weather being favourable the shooting was of a fair description. The range was in charge of Sergeant- Instructor Hancock. The following are those who scored not. less than 10 :—Corporal Kent. Private Wallace, Private Martin,Colour-Sergeant Houghton, Sergeant lkfcCormick,Private Gibson,Sergeant Han- cock, Private Ellg.m, Private L. S. Rimmer, Private Tiorrahin, Private Liptrott, Private Boardman, Ser- geant Bateman, Sergeant Wright, Private Turner, Private Gee. Private Irving,Corporal Pullen,Sergeant Rimmer, Sergeant. Rowse, Corporal Pendlebury, and Privates Wallace, Jackson, and McCoy. The prizes were distributed the same night in the orderly room by Colour-Sergeant Houghton. The committee desire to thank the tradesmen and others who con- tributed to the prize list. RAILWAY ACCIDENT AT EARLESTOWN.

About midnight on Monday a goods train, which had been shunting in the sidings near the Viaduct box at Earlesto,.vn, met with an accident which has had a serious result. It appears when the train had completed the shunting operations, the engine had drawn past the ground disc which re- gulates the trap points, and the driver was thus unable to see whether the points were open or not. The brakesman however gave the signal for right away, and the home signal being off, the driver started for St. Helens. The signal however was for a train on the main line, and the trap points were open, and instead of going on to the main line, the engine ran through the points with some force into the stop block, which it drove about. a dozen feet away, and then ran off the metals down the bank, the wheels becoming embedded in soft ballast to a depth of about two feet. The fireman jumped into the hedge at the side of the line and escaped with- out injury, but the driver stuck to his post. Had the ballast not been soft, the engine would cer- tainly have run down the embankment and pro- bably have overturned. The brakedown gang from Warrington was soon on the scene, but it was not until half-past four on Tuesday afternoon that the engine was re-railed.

NEWTON -DISTRICT COUNCIL.

BUSINESS OF THE NEXT MEETING.

The next meeting of this Council is announced for Monday afternoon next, when the following among other business win be submitted.

HIGHWAYS, PI7BLIC WORKS AND SANITARY COMMITTER.

This Committee's minutes state that a special report had been received from Mr. Greeory, as to irregularities In dealing with the nightsoil belonging to the Council. It is also stated that a long discussion ensued as to whe- ther legal proceedings should be taken against the men, or whether they should summarily be dismissed, and it was resolved .that one man be summarily dismissed. It was resolved that the whole question of the removal of nightsoil, together with the supervision and check upon the men, be referred to the Nightsoil Sub Commit- tee to report thereupon to the Sanitary Committee. The Committee has also resolved that the Council do not allow occupancy of any huts on the Market-square, except on market days. A letter was read from Mr. R. B. Tort, asking for a reduction in the charge for one of the ante-rooms in the Town Hall, for a dancing class, as it was resolved that the ante-loom be let to Mr. R. B. Toft for the winter season, 1e95-6, at the rate of five shillings per night. A letter was read from Lance-Sergeant Bailey. asking for the use of the large room, '&l., for the Ist Battalion South Lancashire Regimental Ball, at reduced prices,and it, was decided that the matter be dealt with by the Council. _

_ Resolved that the committee make application for the hire of a steam roller, and that application be at once made to the Wigan Corporatinu, and failing to obtain the same, that advertisements be issued for the hire of a roller.

A letter from Nir.lßeesley in reference to his account was read, and it was resolved that the question of Mr. Beesley's account be referred to the next committee meeting ; it was also resolved that Mr. Ronayne's letter be referred to the next committee meeting,

GAS AND WATER COMMITTEE:

This committee has reanlved that the Chairman open and inspect the tenders f,r the as works, and that he have authority to order therefore for immediate require- ments, and to report to the next meeting. A letter from Mr. Webb ac. to the water supply was read,and it was resolved that the letter be acknowledged and referred to the sub-committee.

THE LETTING OF PUBLIC ROOXS.

Mr. Evans has given notice that he will move that the present scale of charges for letting the Assembly and other rooms in the Town Hall at Earlestown be, and is hereby altered, so as to allow of a reduction of twenty- five per cent, off the present rates being made in the case of the rooms being taken by ratepayers of the dis- trict.

A ghastly diacovery has been made in the Manchester Town Hall, a watchman finding the body of a gasfittings inspector, named Newton, hanging from a towel rail in the lavatory.

Sunday was Mr. Gladstone's 86th birthday, and, as usual, a large number of congratulatory letters and tele- grams arrived at Hawarden. Owing to his absence on the CiSntinent, however, the number was not so large as in former years. A telegram has been received from Biarritz stating that Mr. Gladstone and his party arrived there 'all well" early on Sunday morning.

NEW YEAR'S DAY AT EARLESTOVVN AND NEWTON.

New Year's day opened in spring-like weather which tempted a large number of people to indulge in outdoor exercise. The previous evening watch- night services were held at most of the places of worship, and were well attended. The streets until well into the new year, were a very busy appearance, a good number of people heralding the advent of the new year in ball rooms. Immediately the hour of twelve had struck, the sounding of whistles proclaimed the birth of a new year. There was nothing during the day to attract people, and consequently a very large number left the town for adjoining towns. In the afternoon Earlestown football club paid a visit to Ilaydock to play off their Alliance fixture with Elaydock the match endiug in a draw of one goal each. In the evening the various school parties were held, particulars of which will be found below. CONGREGATIONAL SUNDAY SCHOOL, NEWTON.

The annual treat was held in the schoolroom on Wednesday evening. There were about 200 to tea, the tables being presided over by the teachers. After tea an entertainment was given by the scholars and others, the Rev. J. Allatt presiding. Mr. M. Battersby, secretary, gave a report for the past year, in which he said that the average attendance of teachers was 15. and scholars 143. The number of magazines bound was 110, comprising good sound healthy literature, and the larger priced volumes were on the increase. The school had lost one or two teachers from various causes, but their places had been to some extent filled by other efficient teachers. The penny bank was doing good service, but it was yet too early to fur- nish a statement to the 31st December, all bank books had been called in to have the interest added, and the annual statement would be furnished a little later on, probably at the annual church meeting. The following programme was given during the evening :—Piano duet, "Sans Sauci," Misses E. M. and C. Taft; song, "Sleep, my love, sleep," Miss G. Allatt ; whistling solo, "Love's golden dream," Mr. J. Howard; quartette, "The stars are with the voyagers," Mrs. Turner. Miss D. Mather, Messrs. J. aria W. Owen; violin solo, Mr. H. Pennington; song, "The daisies," Miss Elsie Brownlee; chorus, "Glory to God," and "Hail golden morning," Junior Christian Endeavour Society ; song, "The star of Bethle- hem," Miss D. Mather; humorous sketch, "The rehear- sal," nine scholars; song, "The yeoman's wedding song," Mr. W. Owen; action song, "Raise the tam- bourine on high." six girls; whistling solo, "The lost chord," Mr. J. Howard ;song, "Killarney," Mrs. Turner; quartette, "0 hush thee my baby," Mrs. Turner, Miss D. Mather, Messrs. J. and W. Owen; recitation, " What I would do," Miss Lily Warren ; song, "Deane," Miss E. Jones. At the close Mr. Allatt distributed the maga- zines which had been bound free of cost.

BAPTIST TEA IPARTY AND ENTERTAINMENT. The annual tea party and entertainment in connection with the Baptist Church was held on Wednesday. There were about 160 present to tea, and the tables were pre- sided over by Mrs. Morris, Misses Limond, Crown and Fail (2). Partington (2), Miss Morris, Miss Holt, Messrs. Morris, Swarisbrick, Godbort, Mai nes, Robinson,Hulme, R. Morris and W. Limond.—The Rev. F. E. Miller pre- sided over the entertainment which followed, and Mr. W. Limond. secretary, submitted his report for the past year, in which he said, the year just closed had been a very successful one, and he thought the teachors, scho- lars and officers alike, could congratulate themselves and wish, on this first day of tha new year, that the present year may be equally successful. The number of scholars had increased from 183 to 198, with an average atten- dance of 84 in the morning and 138 in the afternoon. The teachers and other officers numbered 26. The finances, he was happy to say, were very satisfactory,the balance now in hand being £6 17s. 41., as against Ills. 3d., on December 31st, 1894. The different societies, Christian Endeavour, Band of Hope, and Mutual Improvement, were doing a good work in their 'spheres. The Dorcas class, conducted by Mrs. Shim, was a new institute, the object being, as the name indicates, to provide clothing, confined to the ladies, and the class provided a free breakfast to the poor children on Christmas Day. In the early part of the year they were deprived of the ser- vices of their esteemed superintendent, Mr. Hulme, who through ill health,was compelled to resign. They were, however, fortunate in persuading Mr. Evett to accept the Superintendency, a position he has filled with every satisfaction both to teachers and scholars. It was their intention to form a library in connection with the school, I the advantages of which he was sure would be appreci- ated by all their members. For some years it had been his finishing remark to call attantion to the insufficient accommodation they had at their dispoosal—in fact they were very much cramped for room. He hoped, however, that by this time next year he should have the pleasure of presenting his report in the new premises. (App:ause.) The entertainment was then proceeded with, the fol- lowing nrogramme being efficiently rendered :—Opening glee, "Laugh and grow fat," choir; selection on glasses. Mr. J. Isherwood; recitation "Before a lowly cottage," Miss M. Wakefield; action song, "The British flag." the children ; piano selection, " I dreamt I dwelt in marble halls," Miss A. Clare ; dialogue. "The County Parliament," seven members ; song " Anchored," Mr. W. Limond ; recitation, " Paul Revere's ride," Mr. S. Harvey ; action, song " Song of the flowers," children ; piano duet" In the woodlands," Misses S. A. Fail and E. Partington ; recitation," Death of the Old Year." Miss F. Kenning; song and chorus "The Lord Mayor's Show," Mr. G. Roberts ; dialogue, " The District Council of Christmas," eight members ; song, "Fond heart and true," Miss M. Fail ; selection on glasses, Mr. J. Isher- wood. At the close, the Rev. F. E. Miller distributed 76 nicely bound books to scholars as awards for punctual attendance.

"CHARLEY'S AUNT."

As will be seen from our advertising columns " Char. Lea's Aunt" pays a return visit to St. Helens Theatre Royal next week. It is a remarkable fact that the sun never sets on " Charley's Aunt," for it is now being played in London, the English provinces by two com- panies, in Germany by seven companies, where it is the only piece that ever had the honour of being commanded to be performed at the Imperial Palace before the Em- peror and full Court, in America by two companies, in Australia, and at the Cape. The company visiting St. Helens next week has been carefully selected and re- he.rsed by Mr. Penley himself. The action of the comedy centres around the "spooning' escapades of a couple of chums at an Oxford College, and a visit to tbe college t4v " Charley's Aunt" results in scenes and inci- dents brimful of fun and merriment. Many of the situa- tions are extremely humorous, and the comical cempli- cations which arise therefrom maintain an all-absorbing interest in the performance to its end. MRS. LONGSHORE-POTTS, M.D., AT ST. HELENS. Local ladies will be pleased to see the announcement of the forthcomin,, visit to St. Helens of the well-known Lady physician and lecturer. Dr. Potts, it will be re- membered, lectured to great audiences at the Royal Court Theatre. Liverpool. last December, since then she has been packing to the doors the principal theatres of Scotland and Ireland. Recently, at the Theatre Royal, Shf ffi.-Id, which is also owned by Mr. Walace Revill,pro-- prietor of the St. Helens Theatre, hundreds of women were crowded out at her lectures, and much the same thing is happening on the afternoons of the present week at the New Grand Theaere, Blackpool. Mrs. Dr. Potts is a Qtiakeress. She gradu- ated from the Women's Medical College of Philadelphia early in the fifties—a time when the app arance of a woman i , the medical profession was viewed with small favour. Many rears' experience convinced her that people became ill -largely through want of physicleg,e II knowledge, so fourteen years ago she began de- liverieg lectures in het drawing room to women only. Tkese were so favourably received, that, at the request of the Mayor of Philadelphia and others, she lectured in public to both sexes, and the success cf this experiment induced her to devote all her time to lecturing. She visited all the largest cities of the United states. and in her subsequent tour she appeared in many of the prin- cipal centres of Australia,New Zealand, and India. She will deliver her lectures in St. Helens at the Theatre Royal. that theatre having been booked to her for the five afterreoons comm .ncing next Thursday. The first four lectures will be delivered free of admission,only the final discourse on " Maternity " being charged for. A special feature of the lectures will be the limelight views, which have been described as amongst the finest in the world.

WESTON. WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL.—Tbe Christmastide meet- ings in connection with this selool were concluded on Thursday evening last week, when tea was partaken of by a goodly company. Afterwards a very interesting magic lantern entertainment was given by Mr.Pickthall, 'of Runcorn. The gathering held on the evening of Christmas Day, under the presidency of Mr. J. Ball, also proved successful, the scholars going through a pro- gramme of recitations and dialogues. GENERAL NEWS. In about twenty two seconds a drop of blood goes the round of the body. In about every two minutes the en- tire blood in the body, makes the round through the right side of the heart, the lungs to the left side of the heart, through the asteries, the veins again to the heart.
